replace Optimization-like Reasoning in LLMs over Expanding Search Spaces

Authors: Nicol\'as Astorga, Nabeel Seedat, Mihaela van der Schaar

Abstract: Verifiable reward training has improved mathematical and coding reasoning, but these domains capture only part of step-by-step decision making. Many real-world tasks require finding a high-value feasible plan among many valid alternatives. We introduce OPT*, a scalable family of optimization-style tasks for training and evaluating LLM step-by-step optimization-like reasoning along a complexity axis: each task provides a feasibility checker and evaluator, while a complexity parameter expands the search space without requiring new human labels. This motivates studying these tasks in two regimes: (i) solver-guided online policy optimization, which uses a solver as a value oracle for partial states and applies rank-based reward shaping to reinforce better next steps, and (ii) search-based offline RL when such solvers are unavailable. Theoretically, we relate success in large search spaces to the information a reasoner extracts per unit of search budget. Empirically, we ablate the ingredients that make search efficient on OPT* and show that training on OPT* improves step-by-step optimization-like reasoning.

replace Rhythm of the Deep: Two-tier acoustic organization of sperm-whale codas from click waveforms to coda sequences

Authors: Mudit Sinha, Sanika Chavan

Abstract: Sperm-whale codas are usually described by the number and timing of their clicks. These measures reveal patterns in whale communication, but do not describe how individual clicks sound. We ask what click waveforms add beyond timing and how acoustic information is organized within codas and across sequences of codas. We analyze 1,952 codas, using frozen audio encoders to identify recurring forms of clicks and whole codas independently. Direct spectral and envelope measurements then test whether the click forms differ in recorded sound. Within codas, click-form composition improves the measured prediction of whole-coda form beyond timing alone. Click spacing also remains related to whole-coda sound when the same click forms are present. Neighboring click forms help predict the next click in the annotated sample, although their order does not improve the tested classification of whole codas. Across recorded sequences, the second preceding whole-coda form improves prediction beyond the immediately preceding form. The gain exceeds two broad first-order references, but stricter controls do not establish a general higher-order rule. Together, these findings show different organizing relationships at the two scales. Within codas, click sound and rhythmic spacing help distinguish complete vocalizations. Across codas, earlier whole-coda forms help predict what follows.

replace Latent Goal Prediction from Language for Model-Based Planning

Authors: Samuel Barbeau, Simon Roy, Giovanni Beltrame, Christian Desrosiers, Nicolas Thome

Abstract: Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) enable agents to plan in latent space by imagining the outcomes of candidate actions, yet task specification remains a bottleneck. Visual targets provide precise local gradients but poor distant guidance, while language is flexible yet limited by noisy cross-modal alignment or dependence on distinct large generative models. We introduce LAGO (Latent Goal Prediction from Language), a hierarchical world model in which a single predictor both forecasts action-conditioned dynamics and grounds language instructions as sequences of intermediate latent subgoals, training both modes with a single regression objective over a shared latent space. At each planning step, LAGO predicts a sequence of latent subgoals from a language instruction and optimizes an action sequence using a soft-minimum alignment cost that rewards subgoal proximity without enforcing a rigid path. Subgoals are repredicted as the agent's state evolves, turning a single long-horizon instruction into a sequence of locally tractable objectives. Across environments spanning navigation and manipulation, LAGO outperforms flat and hierarchical image-goal planners, with the largest gains on long-horizon navigation with curved, hazard-constrained paths, where it more than doubles the success rate of a goal-conditioned behavior cloning policy trained on the same demonstrations. Grounding language in the world model's own latent space also outperforms vision-language reward models, including one that plans with ground-truth dynamics.

replace Process Advantage Signal Shaping: Advantage Construction for Process-Supervised Reinforcement Learning in LLM Reasoners

Authors: Chao Wang, Hongtao Tian, Tao Yang, Yunsheng Shi, Ting Yao, Wenbo Ding

Abstract: Dense process supervision provides local evaluations, but leaves open how these scores should become policy advantages. We introduce PASS (Process Advantage Signal Shaping), an advantage-construction method for group-relative policy optimization (GRPO). PASS separately normalizes and fuses reward channels, constructs batch-shared credit units from variation in the fused score trace, and normalizes return-to-go by the remaining unit count. These operations connect feedback scale, credit resolution, and accumulation horizon in a single computation. We evaluate learned process reward model (PRM) feedback for mathematical reasoning and teacher-derived token feedback for multi-hop question answering (QA). Across seven mathematics benchmarks, PASS improves average pass@1 by 4.62 percentage points over outcome-only GRPO with approximately $2\%$ shorter responses. Matched comparisons with Step-GRPO and component ablations further support the choice of shared credit units and residual normalization. On multi-hop QA, PASS improves over process-signal controls across two normalizers; token-level ablations reveal an accuracy--length trade-off: token-level accumulation slightly favors pass@1, while PASS achieves higher pass@8/16 with shorter responses. The results establish the conversion from local process scores to policy advantages as a consequential design choice across distinct supervision sources.

replace RL Post-Training Builds Compositional Reasoning Strategies

Authors: Azwar Abdulsalam, Nishil Patel, Andrew Saxe

Abstract: Does RL post-training merely amplify primitive skills already latent in a base model, or can it compose primitive skills into new higher-level strategies? We study this question in a fully observable rewrite-grammar environment where the pretraining distribution is known and every generated rewrite can be audited. A Transformer is pretrained on primitive symbol-rewrite chains and post-trained on a Trace-based reasoning task with only a binary final-answer reward. RL solves held-out problems that remain rarely solved by the pretrained model even under much larger sampling budgets, while rejection fine-tuning improves early but plateaus. Trace analysis shows that RL reorganizes primitive competence through a phased compositional mechanism: it first strengthens primitive reductions, then discovers valid composed procedures. These include sequential compositions, which collapse ordered chains of primitive contractions, and parallel compositions, which combine independent primitive contractions in a single step. The composed procedures are not isolated samples; they are reused and consolidated into a stable repertoire. Comparing RL with rejection fine-tuning shows that the key difference is not exploration volume but selectivity: RFT produces many shortcut-like rewrites, much of them invalid, whereas RL concentrates exploration into valid reusable structure. Pretraining ablations show that the emergence of compositional strategies is gated not by primitive exposure alone, but by whether pretraining organizes primitive competence into reduction procedures that RL can later compress. The base model provides weak procedural ingredients; RL builds them into reliable higher-level strategies.

replace Test-Time Coverage: Test-Conditioned Data Curation for Deployment-Aware Learning

Authors: Nadine Chang, Maying Shen, Shizhe Diao, Jialiang Wang, Jingde Chen, Thomas Breuel, Pavlo Molchanov, Rafid Mahmood, Jose M. Alvarez

Abstract: Deployed AI systems are often trained from broad candidate data pools, necessitating data curation towards the deployment test distribution. However, standard data curation methods score training-side criteria rather than directly optimizing deployment match. We introduce TTCov (Test-Time Coverage), a data-level test-conditioned curation method that uses test-side information before training instead of updating model weights at inference. TTCov decomposes deployment-conditioned curation into coverage and distribution. To represent coverage, it builds a task Atlas, a collection of LLM-based atomic propositions (APs) describing deployment-relevant concepts, seeded from open task knowledge and expanded with unmatched APs extracted from unlabeled deployment samples. To represent distribution, it instantiates the matched deployment APs with their frequencies, yielding a Knowledge Atlas (K-Atlas) that operationalizes the deployment distribution as a curation target. TTCov then selects a budgeted training set whose deployment APs distribution approximates this target. We apply TTCov towards autonomous driving (AD), keeping adaptation off the inference path while selecting data with greater deployment-relevant coverage, closer K-Atlas matching, and stronger downstream end-to-end driving performance than data-curation baselines, including seamless adaptability to novel domains via city-to-city expansion. Code available at https://github.com/NVlabs/TTCov.

URLs: https://github.com/NVlabs/TTCov.

replace Alternating Abduction and Planning over Temporal Dynamic Knowledge Graphs

Authors: Amir Hossein Sharafi, Alireza Shahbazi

Abstract: Knowledge graphs change over time, but the effects of actions on them are only partly stated: the ontology entails further consequences, facts that an action needs are often unknown, and an action may make the graph inconsistent. We study these problems for Temporal Dynamic Knowledge Graphs (TDKGs), that is, SROIQ knowledge bases whose actions are rules with delete/insert templates and whose events lie on an intrinsic timeline. We present three tools: temporal projection, the exact test of plans; DDA (Direct-Derivation Abduction), which explains an observation by assuming its negation and negating what the reasoner newly derives; and APT (Abduction and Planning over TDKGs), which plans backwards from a goal, assumes the facts that the plan needs but nobody stated, and lets DDA explain the assumptions, each explanation becoming a sub-goal, up to a depth bound. The tools use a SROIQ reasoner only through a small contract, so they depend neither on a particular reasoner nor on a particular data structure; they are designed for a reasoner that processes added and removed axioms incrementally. Change is treated without modal operators, on one linear timeline and without possible worlds, so decidability of projection follows from that of SROIQ. We prove that indirect effects are exactly the entailments of the updated state, that DDA and APT are sound and terminate, and that bounded planning is 2NExpTime-hard and in 3ExpTime. We compare the tools with hitting-set abduction, the solver CATS and classical planning, and run them on six benchmarks and five reasoners with identical results.

replace Explore, Map, Remember, Decide: A Benchmark for VLM Readiness in Safety-Critical Scenarios

Authors: Gabriele La Malfa, Nitay Alon, Emanuele La Malfa, Reuth Mirsky, Stefan Sarkadi

Abstract: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) are often deployed as embodied controllers for robotics, yet it remains unclear how well they can explore an environment, maintain an internal map, and ground safety-critical decisions in long-horizon sequential trajectories. In this work, we introduce EMRD, an evaluation framework that decouples spatial reasoning into four stages: Explore (what the agent observes), Map (what it records and where), Remember (what it retains), and Decide (whether its final choice is justified), scored across 18 stage-specific metrics. We use EMRD to evaluate eleven open- and closed-weight VLMs across 16 buildings under nominal conditions and three environmental stresses (darkness, smoke, and motion noise), where an embodied VLM explores unknown rooms, maps observed objects, and selects the safest gathering place. Open-weight models leave rooms unvisited in 95% of episodes and detect at most 51% of objects; closed-weight models explore more, though with an external memory (notepad), two of the three stop prematurely. Without external memory, most VLMs keep fewer of the objects they saw, especially in larger buildings. The start limits which rooms are explored, and open-weight models started in different rooms agree on the final room not significantly more often than chance. Moreover, these choices are biased by semantic priors: swapping a room's label is often enough to change the final choice. Finally, motion noise increases object position error (by 12-60% for closed-weight models), strong darkness lowers map recall by 28-31 percentage points, and dense smoke reduces seen objects by 25-39 points, while the choice among the rooms entered does not change significantly.

replace Multi-Granularity Context-Enhanced RAG over Multimodal Knowledge Graphs

Authors: Zongyu Wu, Yilong Wang, Xiaochen Wang, Minhua Lin, Zhichao Xu, Fenglong Ma, Xiang Zhang, Suhang Wang

Abstract: Knowledge graph (KG)-based retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) leverages structured knowledge to provide large language models (LLMs) with high-quality external information. Building on these works, recent studies have explored multimodal knowledge graphs (MMKGs) as knowledge bases for GraphRAG. This enables GraphRAG to integrate knowledge across multiple modalities, thereby further enhancing its performance. However, existing MMKG-based RAG methods generally follow a common pipeline in which different modalities are largely processed independently before being fused. As a result, textual context is only used to a limited extent during visual information extraction and subsequent multimodal knowledge fusion. This brings a semantic gap between images and text which limits the multimodal GraphRAG performance. To address this issue, we propose a novel framework for constructing a Context-Enhanced MMKG (CEMMKG) to better support multimodal GraphRAG. The proposed CEMMKG enriches each image with complementary textual context at both local and global scopes. Local context goes beyond the surrounding text by incorporating sentences that are semantically related to the image, while global context provides a summary of the entire document. We further introduce a multi-granularity design for the local context, allowing it to capture semantically relevant information at different levels of detail. Extensive experiments on multiple datasets validate that CEMMKG is effective in leveraging contextual information to improve MMKG-based RAG performance. Moreover, its effectiveness across different MMKG-based RAG methods demonstrates its broad applicability.

replace The Memory Trust Gap: Reliance on Stale Evidence Across Model Scales in LLM Agents

Authors: Jundong Hu, Shekar Ramachandran

Abstract: Persistent-memory agents can follow stale information despite current authoritative evidence. We study stale-evidence reliance across Qwen3 0.6/1.7/4/8B checkpoints using two controlled, action-scored suites: a Benefit suite requiring stored information and a Safety suite providing current evidence as static prompt text. We distinguish stale-value reliance from net accuracy loss relative to no memory. Benefit-suite reliance is 0.92-1.00 across checkpoints. In the Safety suite, larger checkpoints resist a plain stale note but incur substantial harm when it appears newer. A full $2\times2\times2\times2$ factorial manipulates label, recency, source authority, and position. Direct cross-size contrasts identify stronger recency effects in the larger tested checkpoints and a position-effect sign reversal within Qwen3. Metadata improves accuracy at all four checkpoints, with larger gains at 4B/8B; pre-resolution yields near-ceiling accuracy across all four. Llama-Instruct comparisons provide cross-family evidence for selected effects, while RGB and MisBench corroborate stale-value reliance. A framing control shows that the failure extends beyond memory-labeled content. These results characterize cue-dependent vulnerability across tested checkpoints without isolating capability or evaluating a complete memory lifecycle.

replace PhysAlign: A Benchmark for Evidence-Grounded Role Alignment in Multimodal Physics Reasoning

Authors: Kecheng Liang, Haoyang Liu, Zexin Chen, Zirong Liu, Weixing Chen, Qiufeng Wang, Yang Liu, Liang Lin

Abstract: A key challenge in physics diagram understanding is correctly associating visual information with the physical entities, relations, and conditions it describes. Even when a value, symbol, or other local element is accurately recognized, assigning it to the wrong entity or scope can distort the underlying physical premise and lead to incorrect reasoning. To systematically study this challenge, we introduce \textbf{PhysAlign}, a benchmark designed to assess whether multimodal models correctly associate information recognized from physics diagrams with its intended physical role. By disentangling visual recognition from physical-role assignment through localized probes and controlled variants, PhysAlign isolates correspondence errors from recognition failures. It contains 3,341 human-validated probes spanning 986 physics problems, enabling systematic evaluation of visual recognition and physical-role correspondence at scale. We further introduce five complementary evaluation metrics, including CAcc, GAcc, and JAcc, which provide a comprehensive assessment of models' ability to recognize diagram content, establish correct physical correspondences, and solve the underlying physics problem. Across our evaluated multimodal models, PhysAlign reveals a consistent gap between local visual recognition and physical-role grounding. Even when the queried content is correctly recognized, the conditional correspondence error rate remains 13.8\% for GPT-6-Astra and rises to about 50.6\% for InternVL3.5-8B. These findings indicate that strong perception alone does not ensure reliable physical interpretation, exposing a distinct grounding bottleneck that is largely hidden by answer-level accuracy and highlighting the need for future models to better align recognized visual evidence with its physical meaning.

replace Code4Scene: Benchmarking Coding Agents for Constructing and Editing 3D Scenes

Authors: Xiaokang Ye, Siddhant Hitesh Mantri, Zimeng Chen, Edward Zhang, Zhaoxu Zheng, Yuanheng Li, Yizhao Chen, Tianyang Huang, Lianhui Qin

Abstract: Frontier coding agents can now write and execute code that authors 3D environments, but whether they reliably understand 3D structure and precisely control scene state remains unclear. The generated 3D scene is a persistent, executable artifact: a convincing render can hide incorrect spatial relations, intersecting objects, or unintended modifications. We introduce Code4Scene, a benchmark of 170 Unreal Engine cases built from human-assembled scenes that evaluates coding agents on two complementary settings under a shared execution interface. Construction tests scene-level spatial reasoning from open-ended language specifications, where many realizations are valid; editing tests precise control of scene state, where the agent must recover the target scene from reference images while preserving everything else. Rather than scoring code or rendered views, Code4Scene evaluates the generated engine-native scene for task fulfillment, artifact integrity, and static physical validity, with edits additionally compared against withheld ground truth. Across 14 coding-agent systems on the 83-case public set, construction and editing performance are strongly correlated but not interchangeable (Spearman $\rho = 0.67$): Claude Fable 5.1 leads construction, Gemini 3.8 Flash leads editing. Spatial Composition is the weakest construction category for every agent, while editing remains imprecise: the best Repair F1 is only 0.562, and 34.2% of edits that fully recover the target still introduce unintended changes elsewhere in the scene. These results expose a gap between plausible 3D generation and reliable spatial reasoning and state control. The project webpage is at https://simworld.org/code4scene.

URLs: https://simworld.org/code4scene.

replace Mingbird: A Local-First Agent Harness Enabling Small Open Models to Complete Real Tasks

Authors: Hao Wang, Ting Huang

Abstract: Small open-weight language models now run on a laptop, but they fail at multi-step work in ways a large model does not: they stop with the files missing, repeat one action until the budget ends, and report success without checking. At the one-to-nine-billion-parameter tier the harness supplies almost all of the procedural control that a large model supplies for itself.We present Mingbird, an open-source harness for that tier on a consumer graphics card: 12k lines of Python, a 628-test suite, and eighteen mechanisms for context economy, completion, time budget and the host boundary, plus a crawl guard outside those four groups. Its design point is completion control: the harness runs executable checks on the working directory and refuses a claim of completion when they fail.Five evaluations accompany it. A configuration-surface audit finds four defects that the 625-test suite of v3.1.1 missed, each because a test checked a property adjacent to the one that broke. A ten-task probe suite gives each mechanism a reason to fire and reports where the change reaches the artifact, including one mechanism that cuts the signal it protects. Disabling five of six completion-time checks costs 0.152 of the artifact score on eighteen held-out tasks, three replications per cell (95% CI [+0.064, +0.246], Holm p = 0.014). On a 288-cell matrix the harness reaches 0.886 against 0.631, 0.479 and 0.405 for three open scaffolds, and on {\tau} 2 -bench it ranks first or tied first in all three domains against the arms evaluated.Tasks, scorer, source and every per-cell result are public.

replace When Terminal-Agent Training Stalls: Demystifying Data Generation and Verification Challenge

Authors: Xi Qin, Isabel Kurth, Xin Cui, Elin Park, Alexander Schaefer, Yaad Oren

Abstract: Automated terminal-agent training relies on generated environments and verifiers to turn open-ended interaction into reward. Yet a runnable Docker image and executable test suite do not guarantee that the resulting reward is faithful, informative, or even attributable to agent behavior. We present a pipeline that generates containerized terminal tasks and their executable verifiers, and use it to demystify failure modes in task generation and live-environment evaluation. Our analysis separates four requirements that are often conflated: structural validity, verifier faithfulness, policy solvability, and infrastructure reliability. In a GRPO training of Qwen3.5-9B, it overfits on a relatively easy dataset within 20 steps. Conversely, in a controlled Qwen3.5-9B comparison, adding hard tasks lowers mean rollout pass@2 from 81.3% to 20.6%, restoring informative failures without changing the training configuration. We further identify near-duplicate task generation, incomplete anti-shortcut safeguards, Dockerfile failures, asynchronous pass@k bias, and concurrency-induced environment errors as distinct threats to measurement. These findings should motivate the community for a terminal agent specific evaluation harness that pairs structural checks and rollout-derived solvability with extensive verifier audits.

replace Reliable Self-Evolution with Imperfect Proxy Rewards

Authors: Kangjun Noh, Soyu Kim, Kyungwoo Song

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based self-evolving search is a promising approach to scientific discovery. However, high-fidelity evaluation of every candidate is prohibitively expensive in some domains. Self-evolving systems in such settings therefore rely on low-cost but imperfect proxy rewards, which may assign high scores to infeasible candidates. These false positives may contaminate both the final output and the feedback used to guide subsequent generations. This motivates statistically calibrated reward intervals for more reliable self-evolving search. We propose Conformal Interval-Driven Self-Evolution (CISE), which constructs candidate-specific reward intervals using conditional conformal inference and iteration-wise online density-ratio estimation. CISE uses conservative interval-based rewards for evolutionary feedback and returns candidates only when all required property intervals lie entirely within their respective feasible regions. We derive fixed-iteration coverage results under explicit assumptions of independence and covariate shift. We evaluate CISE on three self-evolving search tasks in materials science. In our experiments, all candidates returned by CISE are true positives under high-fidelity evaluation, whereas the baselines return more candidates but include false positives. These results highlight the value of a smaller, more precise shortlist when downstream validation budgets are limited. Our repository is available at https://github.com/MLAI-Yonsei/CISE.git.

URLs: https://github.com/MLAI-Yonsei/CISE.git.

replace Realtime-Bench: Can Frontier Models Solve Real-time Interactive Problems?

Authors: Jingyu Tang, Xu Yang, Zhehao Zhang, Zheng Wang, Qi Cheng, Haozhe Zhao, Manling Li, Wenxuan Li, Xide Xia, Jiaxin Pei

Abstract: Real-time interaction requires agents to perceive evolving environments and act under temporal constraints, yet existing evaluations of real-time streaming models focus on conversational quality and streaming perception, leaving effective action under strict temporal constraints untested. We introduce Realtime-Bench, a suite of carefully curated dynamic tasks where delayed actions directly affect success. Under Wall Clock, where environments advance according to actual inference latency, the best success rate reaches only 44% and the best Pass^3 is only 36%, demonstrating that real-time interaction remains challenging. To separate serving latency from models' real-time streaming capability, we compare Wall Clock with Paused Clock, which removes inference delays as an idealized reference, and introduce Token Clock, which advances simulated time according to generated token counts, testing temporal awareness independently of physical serving speed. We find that temporal costs limit success in evolving environments and that higher reasoning effort has mixed effects under time pressure, reflecting a trade-off between decision quality and timely execution. Based on these results, we discuss future directions for improving real-time multimodal models, including adaptive reasoning with temporal awareness and continuous perception and action.

replace Agentic In-Context Learning: What Algorithms Can Transformers Learn in Closed-Loop Interaction?

Authors: Shiguang Wu, Yaqing Wang, Quanming Yao

Abstract: The learning algorithm view of in-context learning (ICL) studies how fixed-parameter Transformers learn from supplied examples. Language model agents extend this setting through interaction: their actions determine what evidence enters context next. We call this regime Agentic In-Context Learning (Agentic ICL). Existing studies largely depend on externally supplied contexts or reward-bearing interaction histories, while work on general tool-using agents lacks a learning algorithm view. We represent an interactive learner by its state, update, and decision rule, covering active learning, bandits, and online reinforcement learning (RL). For expressivity, we construct causal Transformers that approximate these algorithms over a finite horizon, and bound the resulting loss in return. For training, we characterize when optimizing process rewards preserves behavior that maximizes the final outcome. Distillation experiments show that Transformers can learn to execute three representative recursions. We then train models from outcome or process rewards without oracle action or state targets, as in RL post-training. Different rewards yield distinct decision policies. Improving the alignment of process supervision with outcome-optimal action preferences shifts learned decisions accordingly. Such behavioral differences persist in a pretrained language model across textual paraphrases.

replace A Conditional No-Hair Theorem for Transformers: How Language Models Forget Their Prompt

Authors: Shuyang Xiang

Abstract: A black hole retains only a few conserved charges of the matter it formed from. We are inspired to make the analogy and we ask how much an autoregressive transformer retains of its prompt after generating $t$ tokens. For two prompts followed by the same continuation, the difference of their hidden states decays approximately as a power law $t^{-\alpha}$, where $\alpha=1$ is the rate at which a fixed prompt's share of uniform attention shrinks. Beyond the first layers, trained models forget faster ($\alpha\approx0.55$--$0.7$) than untrained ones of the same architecture ($\alpha\approx0.2$--$0.5$). For finite-depth pre-LN softmax transformers with bounded attention logits and non-degenerate pre-norm activations, we prove that this offset is $O\big(P(\log t)^{L-1}/t\big)$ for prompt length $P$ and depth $L$: the prompt's direct influence is diluted as the context grows, and \emph{relay} through continuation tokens adds only logarithmic factors. Conditional on the generated text, the prompt's influence on the next token thus vanishes. The bound fixes only the asymptotic shape and is uninformative at the lengths we measure in trained networks; an exactly solvable linear relay model shows that it cannot be improved and accounts for the untrained curves. Cancelling the dilution with a logit bias on the prompt stops the decay early in training, and training suppresses relay by about one billion tokens, before attention sinks form. A toolkit that measures $\alpha$ for any Hugging Face causal language model is publicly available.

replace Screening LLM Judges Without Ground Truth: A Preliminary Evaluation of an AI Dental Hygiene Coach

Authors: William DeShong, Barath Kannan, Auberon Palma, Brenda Bradshaw, Ann Bruhn, James F. Leathrum, Jr., Yuzhong Shen

Abstract: Evaluating a fine-tuned coaching model with expert graders is slow and expensive, and an LLM judge cannot be trusted without ground truth. We present a staged pipeline that screens candidate LLM judges and student models before expert review, using explicit decision rules at each stage: judge output audit, similarity with hosted judges, bias checks, and decision robustness. We demonstrate it on an AI dental hygiene coach. The case study uses synthetic, clinically unvalidated training and reference data to demonstrate the operation of the pipeline, rather than to establish the clinical performance of the resulting models. Two fine-tuned small language models (Granite-4.1-8B and Qwen3-8B), each trained at three data sizes, were scored on seven metrics by five judges (three local, two hosted). Two local judges, Qwen3.8-27B and Gemma-4-31B-it, were not distinguishable from the agreement between the hosted judges on six of seven metrics by rank correlation, and Qwen3.8-27B was the most similar to the hosted judges overall. No judge met our criterion for favoring its own model family or training-data source. The student comparison did not separate the two models on any metric under the pipeline's rule, so the choice of Granite-4.1-8B, which led in 29 of 35 judge-by-metric cells, is a lean that depends partly on truncated Qwen3-8B outputs. The pipeline also surfaced problems that raw scores hid, including the truncated outputs and hallucination flags from one hosted judge that no other judge corroborated. No dental hygiene expert has graded these responses, so the findings are preliminary and the pipeline's final stage, anchoring to expert judgement, is proposed but not performed.

replace ABACUS: Program-Guided Multistep Reasoning over Frozen Judgment Models

Authors: Yuhao Wang, Zelin Zang

Abstract: Judgment models answer semantic questions by scoring a finite set of candidate outcomes. Can reusable programs turn these local decisions into powerful multistep reasoning? We introduce \method, a framework that jointly constructs judgment schemas and composition programs for frozen judgment models. A generative teacher designs the procedure offline; the judgment model resolves local semantic questions online, and the saved program composes its decisions into an answer. We study three allocations of design effort between humans and the teacher, focusing on construction through a shared interface. We instantiate the framework with Kev. On three selected BIG-Bench Hard tasks, nine independently constructed programs solve all 450 executions of 150 development inputs. A separate deployment study freezes three programs after development-set reference-agreement refinement and evaluates them on 600 held-out questions. These programs achieve near-perfect 99.67\% macro accuracy, versus 64.00\% for direct Kev and 68.80\% for final-answer-only Qwen3.5-4B. They attain comparable observed accuracy to Qwen's unrestricted response policy (99.07\%) with an \textbf{8.43-fold online speedup}: 0.255 versus 2.150 seconds per question. These results show how reusable programs can substantially expand the reasoning capability of a compact judgment model while keeping its parameters fixed.

replace Same Simulation, Different Evidence: Where Retrieved Tool Output Costs LLM Agents Accuracy

Authors: Ke Zhang, Sahchit Chundur, Jerry Peprah Owusu, Mohammad Javad Abdolhosseini Qomi, Maziar Raissi

Abstract: LLM agents that compute the right answer can still answer wrongly. Agent harnesses increasingly keep long tool outputs out of the context window, saving them to files that the agent searches or reads on demand. We ask where this costs accuracy with a real scientific tool: PHREEQC, a widely used geochemistry simulator from the USGS, whose reports reach 846.8k characters and whose determinism lets every printed value be traced. Agents use it to answer 158 execution-verified multiple-choice items. When agents read the simulator's report through a section index rather than in context, GPT-5.1 and GPT-5.2 lose 11.3 and 7.9 points of accuracy, while Claude Opus 4.6 and Claude Sonnet 4.6 show no detectable cost. Re-running all 11,007 logged simulator calls shows that both GPT models compute the keyed value about as often as before; what changes is the evidence they answer from. Under retrieval they often skip the report, answer from their own higher-precision output files, and misround to a neighboring option, a failure we call evidence substitution. With the simulation held fixed, retrieval still costs GPT-5.2 9.4 points. For GPT-5.4, more frequent correct simulations mask an evidence-use loss of about ten points, leaving a net change indistinguishable from zero. Naming the authoritative output raises accuracy by 6.3-7.9 points over plain retrieval for the three GPT models; for GPT-5.1 and GPT-5.2, inlining outputs that fit works as well, while asking for correct rounding does not. Context-management choices in agent harnesses should be judged by where accuracy is lost, not only by final accuracy.

replace RIPPLE: Recovery Internalization through Process-Level Policy Learning and Effect-Aware Credit for Long-Horizon Agentic Coding

Authors: Jingxuan Xu, Zihang Xu, Yuechen Luo, Ziqi Miao, Wenqiang Zhu, Weihao Li, Junhao Fan, Xinshun Feng, Zhiyi Lai, Yanan Wu, Haoyang Huang, Ken Deng

Abstract: Long-horizon coding agents can observe a failure and generate a plausible critique yet continue along the same incorrect trajectory. We call this the Critique-to-Action Handoff Gap. We introduce RIPPLE, a recovery-oriented training framework that internalizes process-level guidance into a single self-recovering policy. A prefix-only teacher provides evidence-grounded recovery supervision without access to future actions or terminal outcomes. After lightweight distillation, reinforcement learning assigns stage-specific credit to reflection triggering, diagnosis, and corrective action. Local validity signals gate trajectory advantages so that intermediate recovery decisions do not indiscriminately inherit terminal outcomes. Across four repository-level software-engineering benchmarks, RIPPLE improves the base policy by an arithmetic mean of 4.8 score points, with no auxiliary critic at deployment. Controlled ablations isolate the benefits of local, stage-aligned credit, and matched-prefix interventions show that critique semantics affect subsequent correction. Recovery gains concentrate where the prefix contains sufficient diagnostic evidence and the repository state remains reversible within the remaining budget.

replace MatOS: An Agent Operating System for Materials Research

Authors: Dingyang Lyu, Jing Gao, Zhonghao Chang, Jingjie Huang, Xiangran Cheng, Xin He, Yongda Huang, Zhen Lei, Zefeng Li, Jiahui Liu, Jian Wen, Feng Yu, Bonan Zhu, Yongheng Li, Hongwei Du, Baole Wei, Ziheng Lu

Abstract: Materials research relies on scattered and growing data, knowledge, and simulation tools, and a single study often spans multi-day workflows across literature analysis, computation, and experiment. Large language model agents can now retrieve materials data, operate simulation codes, and close design-synthesis-characterization loops with autonomous laboratories. However, extending these agents with new capabilities, keeping long-running tasks on track, and letting them learn from past tasks and user feedback remain difficult. Here we present MatOS, an agent operating system for materials research with three core components: (1) a layered scientific asset graph that organizes data, knowledge, tools, skills, roles, and workflows as reusable assets; (2) a stateful adaptive workflow that runs tasks as explicit execution graphs with scientific checks, so that they can be resumed and traced; and (3) evidence-driven asset evolution, which revises these assets from execution records and user feedback. On ResearchClawBench, MatOS improves on the previously best-performing agent by 11.15% with the same base model and extends the cost-score Pareto frontier. With a common base model, it outperforms Codex CLI and Claude Code, exceeding Codex CLI by 2.13% on MatTools and 4.17% on SciAgentGym. In eight research cases, MatOS narrowed more than 30 million candidate structures to 97 solid-electrolyte candidates, identified 89 halide variants with higher computed conductivity than their parent structures, linked the force errors of 11 pretrained interatomic potentials to local atomic environments, and supported two new methods that reduce errors by 3.1% and 13.6% relative to their baselines. These results show that organizing research capabilities as reusable, evolving assets enables agents to carry out long, multistage materials studies. Code is available at https://github.com/DingyangLyu/MatOS.

URLs: https://github.com/DingyangLyu/MatOS.

replace Skill erosion, individual memory and the reversibility of AI adoption

Authors: Renaud Lambiotte

Abstract: Generative AI can improve immediate performance but may erode the human skills needed for future work. We consider a population of agents who perform a task either with their own skill or with the help of AI. Agents tend to imitate more successful peers, and their skills evolve in time: improving with practice and eroding with reliance on AI. We study, analytically and with simulations, the collective consequences of AI adoption. We show that the mechanism of skill erosion makes adoption bistable and that the long-term behaviour of the population depends on how skills are distributed: populations with the same adoption and mean skill can reach opposite asymptotic states, depending on how expertise is shared between AI users and non-users. A deterministic description that follows the mean skills of the two groups and the variance of skills among non-users predicts the outcome of individual runs as accurately as the simulations allow, whereas the means alone are not sufficient. We also consider the situation when the benefit of AI improves in time. In that case, adoption can jump abruptly, and a temporary increase in benefit, such as in a period of low prices, can leave a population relying on AI after the benefit has returned to its original value. Skill erosion therefore creates a history-dependent switching cost that can make widespread AI adoption difficult to reverse.

replace Corralling AI Risks with Grassroots Contracts

Authors: Ehud Shapiro

Abstract: AI's inflection point is a fountain of risks for digital platforms, as those in control of global platforms cede control of the algorithms, membership and content to AI: (i) AI control of global platforms used by the majority of humanity; (ii) unaccountable AI agents operating anonymously, possibly masquerading as humans, serving unknown masters with unknown goals; and (iii) unattributed deepfakes generated by anonymous AI, which people cannot distinguish from genuine content. Here, we propose to corral these AI risks with grassroots contracts, a digital incarnation of a two-centuries old notion of Pierre-Joseph Proudhon: a social contract that can be freely adopted by any group of people, where groups bound by the same contract can union by mutual consent, possibly into a single global union. Grassroots contracts are realised by grassroots platforms, person-to-person platforms owned, operated and governed by their participants. We developed, with AI, a methodology to compile grassroots contracts to working smartphone apps. We present grassroots contracts for a social graph with accountable AI agents and for a social network with digital provenance, and working smartphone apps that implement the two, and show how they corral these risks: (i) grassroots platforms are not controlled by a third party who may cede control to AI; (ii) AI agents are introduced with accountability: similarly to cars, their manufacturers are accountable for manufacturing defects and their operators for reckless operation; and (iii) every item carries the signatures of its author and of every agent that forwarded it, so creators and forwarders of harmful content are accountable and culpable. Much work remains for grassroots apps to be competitive with incumbent global platforms; but perhaps now this could be achieved with some AI-months rather than with incalculably-many person-years.

replace Zero-Shot Weight-Space Programming Across a Difficulty-Graded Ladder of Two-Player Games

Authors: Eduardo C. Garrido-Merch\'an

Abstract: Weight-space programming sets every weight of a feed-forward network by hand, from an explicit specification of the desired computation, with no gradient descent and no training data anywhere in the construction. We ask how far a large language model can carry this practice, zero-shot, across games of deliberately increasing difficulty. We design a four-rung ladder (Nim, tic-tac-toe, two sizes of Connect Four, and a chess endgame) spanning four axes of difficulty, and build a second, independent network at each rung, using a structurally unrelated game at matched difficulty, to separate what a rung's finding says about the difficulty level from what it says about the one game used to measure it. Ten networks are built and independently verified. Six admit a complete, non-statistical proof of correctness against an independent oracle. The chess-endgame rung is exact where correctness is cheap, move legality and, in one network, an exact one-ply mate detector, and collapses where it is expensive, composing a multi-move mating plan, from 84.1-100% success at the one-ply tactics it was built to get right down to 0.57-9.13% in full adversarial play; its replicate shows this collapse is an order of magnitude less severe under a shorter required plan and exact rather than heuristic safety checks. The standard-sized rung confirms, by holding hand-set evaluation weights fixed and adding only shallow search, that the remaining gap is an architectural ceiling rather than a matter of engineering effort, and this exact collapse reproduces across two structurally unrelated games. We release all ten networks, their independent oracles, construction-cost measurements, and a cold-prompt protocol for a future cross-model comparison

replace Lightfoot: Graph Retrieval-Augmented Generation with Exact, Model-Free Document Deletion

Authors: Uday Allu (Yellow.ai)

Abstract: Graph-based retrieval-augmented generation (GraphRAG) improves multi-hop question answering, but deployed systems pay costs that research comparisons rarely measure: model calls at query time, building the graph, and removing documents correctly. We present Lightfoot, a GraphRAG system in which every entity, link, fact and synonym edge is a deterministic function of per-passage extractions that carry chunk-level provenance. Retrieval makes no language-model call, indexing makes one per passage, and deleting a document is a database transaction with no model calls: 14 seconds for 10% of an 11,402-passage corpus, after which the graph is identical to a fresh index of the surviving documents. On a corpus of near-duplicate names, HippoRAG 2's capped synonym lists leave 565 fewer edges than a rebuild, while Lightfoot remains exact. Under identical extraction, embedding and answer models on held-out questions of MuSiQue, 2WikiMultiHopQA and HotpotQA, Lightfoot is competitive with HippoRAG 2: with Holm-corrected paired tests it is better on MuSiQue at recall@5 and recall@10 (+4.8 and +5.5 points) and on 2WikiMultiHopQA at recall@10 (+3.4), and tied on HotpotQA, while HippoRAG 2 keeps a non-significant 3.0-point edge at 2WikiMultiHopQA recall@2. Seeding the walk from entities named in the question accounts for most of the 2WikiMultiHopQA gain. Answers tie on all three datasets, and the retrieval pattern holds with three larger embedders, up to Qwen3-Embedding-8B. The gain is not the reranker's: with the same cross-encoder, dense retrieval trails Lightfoot by 9.3 and 17.8 points recall@5 on the two multi-hop sets. Lightfoot also builds the cheapest index, 26-31% below HippoRAG 2; LightRAG needed 2,187 model calls and 3.7 hours for the same deletion, and fast-graphrag cannot delete.

replace SkillEarth: Teaching General-Purpose Agents to Act on Earth via Skill Evolution

Authors: Haojun Tang, Wenda Zhao, Xiang Zhou, Qiwei Lin, Lida Huang, Yan Wang

Abstract: Recently developed remote sensing (RS) agents execute real-world tasks with complex workflows through multi-step tool invocation. These agents, however, typically rely on predefined expertise or parameter updates. They thus require updating expert knowledge or retraining models whenever new tools, tasks, or execution patterns emerge, limiting their continual adaptability in dynamic Earth environments. In this work, we seek to equip general-purpose agents with RS tool-call knowledge that can be automatically extracted from demonstrated trajectories without expert intervention or parameter updates. Specifically, we propose SkillEarth, a self-evolving skill extraction method that externalizes RS tool-call knowledge as reusable, retrievable, and evolvable skill files and refines them through observation-grounded evolution. Unlike existing automatic skill extraction methods that rely on outcome-based reflection to refine skills holistically, SkillEarth grounds skill evolution in real tool-chain execution and combines dual-judge feedback with a grounded observation routing graph for precise component-level refinement. The resulting skills are generalizable, reliable, and transferable: they improve overall RS tool-call performance across three frontier LLMs and four open-source LLMs, are refined through real tool execution feedback, and support cross-model reuse without retraining. Experiments on OpenEarth-Bench and Earth-Bench demonstrate that SkillEarth achieves better tool selection, argument correctness, execution-order consistency, and task completion than existing RS agents and skill extraction methods.

replace Zero-Shot Weight-Space Programming for Prediction and Classification of Target Variables in Structured Datasets

Authors: Eduardo C. Garrido-Merch\'an

Abstract: Weight-space programming fixes every parameter of a feed-forward network by hand, with no gradient descent anywhere in the construction. Every demonstration so far starts from a target function handed to the model as a rule system, making it a compilation problem. A supervised dataset withholds that specification, so the procedure must do the statistical inference itself and then express the result in the parameterisation it was given. We ask whether a large language model can do both. It is shown a small dataset in full, with the architecture of a feed-forward network, and writes out every weight and bias as a literal number, with no optimizer, no gradient, no code execution, and no chance to evaluate it first. Each construction is sealed and pre-registers its expected score. The graded suite runs from two toy problems, through nine synthetic problems disguised by a hidden rotation, to the classical benchmarks and one real engineering regression. The frozen network matches the closed-form optimum wherever that optimum is linear, reaches the irreducible error of the generating process on three rungs where it is not, and beats every fitted baseline on two of those, including the same architecture trained by gradient descent. It fails on two rungs, both times because the structure to be found is oblique to the coordinates it analysed. Three further constructors separate two failure modes a single score conflates: one returns no parameter vector at all on the hardest rungs, another delivers everywhere while writing networks worse than predicting the mean. One of those failures proves avoidable: another constructor solves the oblique-hinge rung by a margin that survives multiple-comparison correction. What predicts whether the hidden layer earns its keep is the training error of a linear separator, which the composite complexity index misses.

replace Information Limits of Replay-Free Recurrent State Migration

Authors: Khizar Qureshi

Abstract: When a recurrent model is replaced, its old state may not determine what the new model would compute from the same history. We characterize the information that must be retained before the replacement is known. For a time-invariant affine linear family, the minimum continuous causal memory for order-$k$ local migration equals the reachable dimension of a lifted coefficient system minus the information in the anchor state. A controllable $d$-state model with scalar input needs at most $kd$ additional real coordinates, even when arbitrary matrix replacement directions are revealed only after streaming. A single rank-one direction can already require all $kd$. Conditional singular values and auxiliary-bit bounds distinguish this local information law from recovery at a fixed tolerance. We also give invariant-subspace and future-output constructions. Controlled experiments compare the resulting representations. In a small language model, equal-memory cubic interpolation improves mean state fidelity over third-order coefficients but provides no meaningful predictive gain. An analytic history basis preserves useful adaptation more economically in a coupled identification task. With repeated slow poles, eight history coordinates meet normalized output error $10^{-4}$ across five fitted seeds despite substantial full-state error. Splitting the poles raises the smallest tested successful width to 64.

replace Regularizing Treatment Leakage for LLM-based Uplift Estimation

Authors: Bojing Feng, Jinqiu Deng, Zihan Hong, Yuan Xue, Wenqi Xiang, Shuhan Wang, Feng Xiao, Shiwei Wang, Zijiang Liu, Shaomin Li, Yongzhi Qi

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) provide semantic features for uplift estimation, but treatment-revealing text can induce prediction shortcuts that do not transfer to deployment. Controlled proxy experiments provide exploratory evidence of improved ranking scores under injected treatment information relative to no regularization and the tested weight-decay setting. Structured-only benchmarks show modest, dataset-dependent effects. A separate one-week online A/B test of the complete deployed pricing policy records $5.45\%$ higher sales volume and $1.91\%$ higher gross merchandise value than the control arm. Abstract: Achieving full automation in self-driving vehicles remains a challenge, especially in dynamic urban environments where navigation requires real-time adaptability. replace-cross Evaluating and Mitigating Skin Color Bias in Skin Lesion Classificatio

Authors: Kuniko Paxton, Koorosh Aslansefat, Dhavalkumar Thakker, Yiannis Papadopoulos

Abstract: Skin lesion classifiers may overlook performance disparity because of subtle skin tone variations by treating skin tone as a categorical attribute. This study proposes a method that represents skin tone as a continuous attribute and assigns individual weights to each sample based on its nonlinear relationship with classification performance. Evaluations on two dermatological datasets showed that fairness metrics were sensitive to skin-color categorization. The proposed method also demonstrated more stable fairness improvements than conventional categorical reweighting while generally preserving classification performance.

replace-cross Recursive Learning-Based Virtual Buffering for Analytical Global Placement

Authors: Andrew B. Kahng, Yiting Liu, Zhiang Wang

Abstract: Due to the skewed scaling of interconnect versus cell delay in modern technology nodes, placement with buffer porosity (i.e., cell density) awareness is essential for timing closure in physical synthesis flows. However, existing approaches face two key challenges: (i) traditional van Ginneken-Lillis-style buffering approaches are computationally expensive during global placement; and (ii) machine learning-based approaches, such as BufFormer, lack a thorough consideration of Electrical Rule Check (ERC) violations and fail to "close the loop" back into the physical design flow. replace-cross QUTCC: Quantile Uncertainty Training and Conformal Calibration for Imaging Inverse Problems

Authors: Cassandra Tong Ye, Shamus Li, Tyler King, Kristina Monakhova

Abstract: While deep learning offers tremendous promise for scientific and medical imaging, any failures and hallucinations (predictions that do not coincide with reality) are hard to pinpoint and can have serious downstream consequences. Uncertainty estimation techniques, such as conformal prediction, can help by predicting statistically valid error bars for a model's prediction. However, popular conformal prediction methods were not designed for high-dimensional image-valued problems and do not take into account spatial correlations within an image during conformal calibration, resulting in larger-than-necessary uncertainty intervals. We propose a practical simultaneous quantile regression method that enables non-linear, spatially-adaptive scaling during conformal calibration. Our method, QUTCC uses a U-Net architecture with a quantile embedding to learn a full conditional quantile distribution during training, and then leverages this non-linear, learned function for spatially-adaptive conformal calibration. At test time, our method can efficiently estimate uncertainty intervals with pixel-marginal coverage guarantees. In addition, QUTCC can also predict pixel-wise conditional probability density estimates without any built-in distributional assumptions. We evaluate our method on several denoising problems, accelerated magnetic resonance imaging, and quantitative phase microscopy. Our method consistently produces tighter uncertainty intervals than prior conformal methods at the same coverage level, can predict plausible conditional distributions for different tasks, and in some cases, high-uncertainty regions can help us locate hallucinations in a model's prediction.

replace-cross TAR: Temporal Anchor-Constrained Reasoning for Video Temporal Grounding

Authors: Chaohong Guo, Xun Mo, Yongwei Nie, Fei Ma, Xuemiao Xu, Chengjiang Long

Abstract: Video Temporal Grounding (VTG) aims to localize specific video segments corresponding to natural language queries. While recent Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) employ Reinforcement Learning to generate Chains-of-Thought (CoT), they typically rely solely on outcome-based supervision. Consequently, this often leads to hallucinations, where the reasoning process becomes disconnected from the visual content and the final prediction. Existing attempts to mitigate this by relying on external supervision from larger models or separate reward models are computationally expensive and prone to rigid patterns. To address these challenges, we propose TAR (Temporal Anchor-Constrained Reasoning), a framework that introduces the temporal anchor (T-anchor) as a transparent and auditable checkpoint mechanism. T-anchor enforces progressive refinement within the CoT, compelling the model to continuously ground its intermediate thoughts in visual evidence and iteratively calibrate temporal predictions, thereby significantly enhancing the faithfulness and autonomy of the reasoning process and final accuracy. Furthermore, we introduce a bootstrapping paradigm that automatically harvests high-quality CoT data using only a standard 7B model, eliminating the dependency on ultra-large models. Extensive experiments demonstrate that TAR achieves state-of-the-art performance and generates faithful, autonomous, and progressively refined reasoning traces.

replace-cross The Economics of AI Foundation Models: Openness, Competition, and Governance

Authors: Fasheng Xu, Xiaoyu Wang, Wei Chen, Karen Xie

Abstract: Foundation model developers choose how open to make their models. Greater openness lowers downstream adaptation costs but can also make competing models easier to use. In a two-period model, an incumbent chooses its license fee and openness, a deployer adapts the model for users, and an entrant offers a competing model. Usage builds model-specific experience that lowers the deployer's future adaptation cost, creating a data flywheel that favors continued use of the incumbent. We identify conditions under which optimal openness falls and then rises as learning strengthens. The incumbent moves from full openness and a high fee to restricting openness for retention, then to a low fee that builds enough usage and experience to retain the deployer despite greater openness. A full-openness mandate can induce the incumbent to abandon this low-fee strategy. The higher fee can reduce first-period adaptation despite greater openness. An openness trap arises when this welfare loss exceeds the second-period gain from easier adaptation of the entrant's model. The trap can disappear when experience transfers across models. Full integration eliminates the fee between the incumbent and deployer but commits deployment to the incumbent, potentially forgoing a rival that is cheaper to adapt. Integration raises their joint profit, consumer surplus, and welfare when learning is sufficiently strong, but can lower them when learning is weak. A small usage subsidy can benefit both developers, the deployer, and consumers. It can instead induce a fee increase larger than the subsidy and reduced openness, raising the deployer's net payment per use and adaptation costs. The resulting reduction in adaptation lowers welfare even before subsidy expenditure is deducted. These findings connect AI policy to developers' pricing and openness responses and the portability of deployment experience.

replace-cross Cross-Epoch Bias in SGD-based Leave-One-Out Influence: Analysis and Accumulative Correction

Authors: Yunxiao Shi, Shuo Yang, Yixin Su, Rui Zhang, Min Xu

Abstract: Training-data influence estimates support data selection and cleansing by predicting the effects of removing individual examples. The standard multi-epoch SGD-Influence Estimator (SGD-IE) aggregates contributions from a sample's repeated appearances along the recorded trajectory. When used to approximate finite deletion, it omits an interaction: later removals act on the parameter displacement accumulated from earlier removals. We introduce the Accumulative SGD-Influence Estimator (ACC-SGD-IE) to account for how earlier removals change the effect of removing the same sample again. It uses the sample's Hessian at subsequent appearances to correct the estimated parameter change. Our non-convex analysis shows that this correction removes SGD-IE's additional interaction contribution from the finite-horizon error bound: the omitted interaction produces a local error linear in the accumulated parameter change, while the remaining Taylor remainder is bounded quadratically under Lipschitz-Hessian assumptions. The bounds characterize how training propagates the remaining error. In non-convex experiments across clean and noisy tabular, text, and vision datasets, ACC-SGD-IE reduces validation-loss-change RMSE by 10.8% and improves Jaccard@10% by 15.2% on average relative to SGD-IE. These gains also translate into lower test misclassification rates after data cleansing on MNIST and CIFAR10 at the evaluated removal budgets. On noisy industrial datasets, it reduces aggregate parameter RMS error with a mean relative reduction of 53.4%. We further discuss computational tradeoffs, provide complementary theoretical analysis and experimental evaluation for strongly convex objectives, and explore potential extensions of the accumulative correction mechanism to other influence estimators.

replace-cross From Strategy Search to Policy Learning: MCTS-Guided Adversarial Co-Evolution for LLM Safety Alignment

Authors: Xurui Li, Kaisong Song

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) increasingly power web-facing applications, including conversational assistants and content services, where open user interaction exposes them to diverse safety threats and adaptive attacks. In particular, jailbreak attackers repeatedly reformulate harmful requests to bypass safety alignment. Although existing studies have concentrated on defending dynamic jailbreaks, they often retain discovered attacks as flat samples and discard information about how those attacks were found. In this paper, we introduce MACE, an MCTS-guided adversarial co-evolution framework that turns search into policy-learning signals for both an attacker and a defender. Its search component, GS-MCTS, explores multi-step compositions of rewriting operators and associates discovered attacks with empirical success frequency and path depth. Its optimization component, AC-TGPO, uses these statistics for difficulty-aware group-relative credit assignment and constructs a temporally asymmetric curriculum from current interactions, pairings between the latest attacker and preceding defender, and attacks that survive a defense update. Thus, MACE connects search structure to both within-round policy optimization and cross-round memory, rather than using MCTS only to generate attacks or treating each round as a flat adversarial dataset. Removing difficulty-aware weighting, asymmetric samples, or persistent hard samples increases defense ASR-LR by 3.7 %, 6.0 %, and 5.0 %, respectively. Across evaluated models, the resulting attacker achieves the highest ASR-LR in all six model--dataset settings, while the defender reduces average ASR-LR from 11.9 % for the best baseline to 8.8 %.

replace-cross Intra-Agent Motion and Inter-Agent Relation Learning for Map-Free Trajectory Prediction

Authors: Wenyi Xiong, Jian Chen, Wenhua Chen, Ziheng Qi

Abstract: Trajectory prediction is essential for safe planning and decision-making in autonomous driving. Map-free prediction improves deployment flexibility by removing the dependence on high-definition maps, but it also loses explicit road-topology and lane-connectivity priors. Consequently, the model must recover stable motion cues from short histories while resolving prospective interaction relations without lane-level guidance. To address these challenges, this paper proposes a map-free trajectory prediction framework centered on a novel Goal-Conditioned Expert-Routed Transformer (GCERT). A lightweight intra-agent front-end first decomposes projected historical motion features into a smooth trend and a local detail residual through a centered moving average. Component-specific residual projections transform the two signals before a causal temporal Transformer summarizes their recombined evolution. GCERT employs dual-level expert routing: edge-level relation experts generate interaction-specific messages, while node-level state experts adapt the transformation after message aggregation. Predicted motion goals update the node representations and construct prospective relative-geometry and orientation edge features before the final interaction layer, thereby conditioning attention, relation-specific message generation, and state transformation. GCERT therefore determines not only which neighboring agents should be attended to, but also what relation-specific information should be transmitted and how the aggregated node state should be transformed. The resulting representations are decoded into multimodal future trajectories and jointly optimized with trajectory-regression, mode-classification, and goal-prediction objectives.

replace-cross The Compute ICE-AGE: OPAL ONE, Continuity That Persists While Reasoning Is Idle

Authors: R. Jay Martin II

Abstract: The Compute ICE-AGE separates persistent application memory from probabilistic reasoning. We present OPAL, a CPU-resident C++17 graph engine that preserves record identities, relationships, and accepted numerical state between reasoning sessions. Applications evolve that state through defined updates, save canonical checkpoints, and recover continuity after restart. Bounded Local Generator Classes (BLGC) provide the mathematical foundation for this bounded local evolution. On Apple Silicon M2 Pro hardware, the current-engine resident-update P50 measured 0.083 microseconds at 100,000, 1,000,000, and 5,000,000 retained Atomic Memory Vertices (AMVs). A same-session 1M-5M-1M sequence reproduced that median using one frozen binary and 1,024 prepared targets, with every process passing its correctness checks. These measurements cover complete resident-update calls, including validation, indexed lookup, and numerical state mutation. Initialization and storage-growth costs are reported separately. Reliability tests verified event replay, conflict rejection, concurrent calls, allocation-failure handling, and fresh-process recovery with continued operation. Engine-version comparisons produced byte-identical checkpoints and continuation files. The installed PYAMV Python package passed 17 SDK contract checks and 20 workload checks. KINDRED's Model Context Protocol interface passed 22 distinct focused checks covering memory operations, authorization, and transport. Together, these results demonstrate persistent continuity with low recurring local-update costs through five million retained AMVs, providing working interfaces for applications and developers. The architecture keeps accepted memory available while reasoning is idle, allowing applications to invoke models only when tasks require interpretation, planning, or generation.

replace-cross Towards Reliable Local Security Agents: Verifiable Post-Training for Linux Privilege Escalation

Authors: Philipp Normann, Andreas Happe, J\"urgen Cito, Daniel Arp

Abstract: LLM agents are increasingly important in security, yet leading systems are typically closed-source and cloud-hosted, limiting reproducibility and their use with sensitive data. Abstract: Accurate transcription of handwritten mathematics is crucial for educational AI systems, yet current benchmarks fail to evaluate this capability properly. replace-cross CyBiasBench: Benchmarking Bias in LLM Agents for Cyber-Attack Scenarios

Authors: Taein Lim, Seongyong Ju, Munhyeok Kim, Hyunjun Kim, Hoki Kim

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as autonomous agents in offensive cybersecurity. In this paper, we reveal an interesting phenomenon: different agents exhibit distinct attack patterns. Specifically, agents exhibit distinct attack-selection biases, differing in both the families they explore and the concentration of their efforts. These patterns remain relatively stable across prompt variations. To systematically quantify this behavior, we introduce CyBiasBench, a comprehensive 630-session benchmark that evaluates five agents on three targets and four prompt conditions with ten attack families. We identify explicit bias across agents, with different dominant attack families and varying entropy levels in their attack-family allocation distributions. Such bias characterizes agent behavior within the evaluated environments and provides information beyond aggregate attack success rates. Furthermore, our experiments reveal a bias momentum effect, where agents resist explicit steering toward attack families that conflict with their bias. This forced distribution shift does not yield measurable improvements in attack performance. To ensure reproducibility and facilitate future research, we release an interactive result dashboard at https://trustworthyai.co.kr/CyBiasBench/ and a reproducibility artifact with aggregated session-level statistics and full evaluation scripts at https://github.com/Harry24k/CyBiasBench.

replace-cross iMuon: Intrinsic Spectral Optimization on Riemannian Matrix Manifolds

Authors: Yibang Li, Bihari Lal Pandey, Ravi Sah, Andi Han, Cyrus Mostajeran, Pratik Jawanpuria, Bamdev Mishra

Abstract: Muon and related norm-constrained optimizers, now central to large-scale learning, compute updates via a linear maximization oracle (LMO) over a matrix-norm ball in Euclidean space. However, they do not generalize cleanly to manifold-valued parameters such as low-rank factorizations, orthogonality constraints, or symmetric positive-definite (SPD) matrices. Naively restricting the Muon LMO to the tangent space (i) breaks quotient symmetries and (ii) couples the tangent-space constraint with an ambient norm bound, which obstructs closed-form solutions. We resolve both issues with a key observation: every Riemannian metric canonically lifts a unitarily invariant Euclidean norm to an intrinsic norm on each tangent space. The resulting intrinsic LMO is symmetry preserving and, under a structural condition on the scaled tangent space, reduces to a standard Euclidean LMO. Building on this, we introduce intrinsic Muon (iMuon), a unified framework with closed-form updates on the fixed-rank, SPD, Stiefel, and Grassmann manifolds for various unitarily invariant norms, including the spectral, Frobenius, and nuclear norms. We establish deterministic and stochastic convergence rates of iMuon with constants that depend only on the manifold dimension. Notably, on the fixed-rank manifold, the constant depends only on the rank, which makes the rate independent of factor conditioning and removes the runtime factor rescaling required by prior work. Experiments on LoRA fine-tuning of LLMs, image classification, and subspace learning illustrate the efficacy of the proposed approach.

replace-cross AgentLens: Revealing The Lucky Pass Problem in SWE-Agent Evaluation

Authors: Priyam Sahoo, Gaurav Mittal, Xiaomin Li, Shengjie Ma, Benjamin Steenhoek, Pingping Lin, Yu Hu

Abstract: Evaluation of software engineering (SWE) agents is dominated by a binary signal: whether the final patch passes the tests. This outcome-only view treats a principled solution and a chaotic trial-and-error process as equivalent. We show that this equivalence is empirically false. We evaluate 2,614 OpenHands trajectories from eight model backends on 60 SWE-bench Verified tasks. We introduce AgentLens, a framework for process-level assessment of SWE-agent trajectories, and release AgentLens-Bench, a dataset of 1,815 trajectories annotated with quality scores, waste signals, divergence points, and 47 task-level Prefix Tree Acceptor (PTA) references. AgentLens builds PTA references by merging multiple passing solutions for the same task, and uses a context-sensitive intent labeler to assign actions to Exploration, Implementation, Verification, or Orchestration based on trajectory history rather than tool identity alone. On AgentLens-Bench, the quality score separates passing trajectories into Lucky, Solid, and Ideal tiers and further decomposes Lucky Passes into five recurring mechanisms. Across the eight model backends, Lucky rates range from 0.5% to 23.2%, and some models move by as many as five rank positions when ranked by quality score instead of pass rate. We publicly release AgentLens-Bench and the AgentLens SDK at https://github.com/agentlens-framework/agentlens, with a project page at https://agentlens-framework.github.io/agentlens/.
