On the 500-image Cityscapes validation set, Semantic Prism achieves 72.07% mean mIoU, improving on direct-interface decoding by 11.39 percentage points. Extensive capacity-matched ablations show higher mean mIoU for multilevel refinement than for flat and single-level alternatives. The Cityscapes-trained model transfers to ACDC Dataset, achieving 46.89% mIoU without target-domain adaptation. Beyond Cityscapes, on BDD100K dataset, a separately trained model achieves 62.22% mIoU on its full 1,000-image validation set. Across the 3 evaluated datasets, C-IHD improves pixel-error area under the AUPR over uncertainty derived from maximum softmax probability, with segmentation predictions held fixed for each comparison. In particular, on ACDC, AUPR increases from 0.6580 to 0.7557, demonstrating that Semantic Prism presents more effective error ranking under adverse-condition domain shift.

replace-cross One Frozen Simulator Is Not Enough: Simulator Collapse in Multi-Agent RL

Authors: Simon Yu, Nicholas Tomlin, Marwa Abdulhai, Ximing Lu, Derek Chong, Abe Hou, Dilara Soylu, Sergey Levine, Christopher D. Manning, Weiyan Shi

Abstract: Multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) for human-AI interaction usually trains the agent against a single large language model (LLM) that simulates the user. We show that agents trained this way can systematically fail to generalize, and we trace the failure to simulator collapse: the simulator LLM is mode-collapsed, and a policy trained against it learns narrow strategies that exploit the simulator's mode behavior; these strategies transfer poorly to unseen simulators and to real users. We formalize the collapse and propose two solutions. Verbalized Sampling, applied at inference time, samples each simulator reply from a verbalized response distribution. Co-Training, applied during training similar to self-play, co-evolves the simulator as the policy, so the policy cannot exploit the mode behavior. A population variant samples the simulator for each rollout from a pool of recent checkpoints. We validate both solutions on three benchmarks: Persuasion for Good, tau^2-bench, and CooperBench. Verbalized Sampling improves held-out success by up to 9% over single-simulator RL, and Co-Training pushes gains further to 14%; the human study shows similar gain on real users. Both solutions also preserve the policy diversity that collapses under single-simulator RL. Overall, our findings on simulator collapse point to diverse simulators as a requirement for RL agents that serve real users. We release SCOPE, an open-source framework for Population Co-Training in multi-agent RL.

replace-cross Query Timing Produces Opposite Positional Biases Between LLMs and Humans

Authors: Jasin Cekinmez, Addison J. Wu, Thomas L. Griffiths

Abstract: Positional biases such as recency and primacy effects have been documented in large language models (LLMs), yet the underlying mechanism by which these models make their evaluations remains poorly understood. Both primacy and recency biases have been observed in human judgments in response to evidence, but recent work suggest that when the listener updates their beliefs - during the presentation of evidence or only at the end - influences the presence of such effects. We investigate whether a similar phenomenon holds for LLMs, finding divergence from human behavior. These biases are more pronounced in newer models compared to their predecessors.

replace-cross Sampling Luck Masquerades as Allocation Gain: Auditing Test-Time Budget Allocation for Neural Combinatorial Optimization

Authors: Jinhyung Bae

Abstract: Neural combinatorial optimization (NCO) solvers report the best of many sampled solutions per instance, and the sample count is, by convention, identical for every instance. Whether a non-uniform allocation of a fixed total budget would buy anything has not been measured. We measure it, and we audit the measurement itself. First, on in-distribution workloads the allocation headroom is not detectable. Across three pretrained solvers (POMO, AM, SymNCO) on uniform TSP-100, an oracle allocation computed and evaluated on the same stored samples reports a 2.2-2.6% gain with intervals excluding zero; measured out of sample the same gain is indistinguishable from zero (0.457, 0.015, -0.512 percent). Following the customary in-sample procedure, all three solvers would have supported a published 2%-level gain that does not exist. We calibrate this bias against an instance-wise null in which the true gain is zero by construction; over the ranges we test it does not shrink with more samples or more instances. Second, the same correction that removes the phantom gains preserves a real one. Under distribution shift (a workload mixing uniform and clustered instances), a pre-registered confirmatory experiment finds that allocation guided by held-out sample statistics improves best-of-k by 11.5% (AM, primary endpoint; 95% CI [7.4, 19.7]) and 12.0% (SymNCO, replication) at equal evaluation budget, with the signal-acquisition cost not charged; a pre-registered negative control (POMO, an order of magnitude more robust to shift) shows -0.3% [-0.7, 0.24]. The gain exceeds a frozen distribution-label baseline by 4.2 points [1.9, 7.7]. An exploratory policy charging a 20-sample probe against the same budget retains 3.4% (AM) and 4.6% (SymNCO). We give a correction procedure and a reporting checklist, and release all data, code, and the pre-registration record.

replace-cross Why ML-based cough models do not generalize: a systematic cross-dataset evaluation for tuberculosis screening

Authors: Wensi Zhang, Tomas Teijeiro, J\'er\^ome Thevenot, David Atienza

Abstract: Cough acoustics are promising for non-invasive tuberculosis (TB) screening, yet whether machine learning (ML) models capture disease-related acoustics or artifacts of data collection remains unresolved. We evaluated the cross-dataset generalizability of classical ML and deep learning (DL) cough-based TB classifiers across three independent datasets. Despite moderate within-dataset performance (ROC-AUC up to $0.76 \pm 0.06$), both pipelines fail to generalize, with external performance frequently below 0.6, indicating a possible limitation of the data. We further observed that audio representations are organized by recording device and dataset rather than TB status, predicted TB probability tracks country-level prevalence in CODA, and device mismatch degrades transfer while device-diverse training improves it. Additionally, a clinical-variable baseline generalizes more consistently (ROC-AUC $0.65-0.71$), indicating acquisition-specific variability in acoustics is a stronger driver of poor generalizability. High within-dataset performance is not enough. External validation is essential before cough-based TB models are clinically ready.

replace-cross Learning Complementary Routes for Scouting Persimmon Orchards during Shoot Growth with CoverShare

Authors: Junhao Cao, Tao Wang, Zipeng Xie, Hongyi Xia, Jianian Wu, Lixia Huang

Abstract: At the study orchard, scouting during persimmon shoot growth focuses on leaf color change, leaf area, and internode length. Several routes may share an access lane, yet each can offer a view of row segments that the others miss. We introduce CoverShare to learn complementary routes under a fixed action budget. Within Policy Gradient for Parallel State Entropy maximization (PGPSE), CoverShare redistributes auxiliary rewards according to the state coverage lost when each policy is omitted and the number of policies visiting each state. It preserves the pooled entropy objective and total auxiliary reward. We simulated the annotated road network of a 6.7-ha site with 107 observable persimmon scouting units across five zones. At 40 actions per route, six distinct policies covered an expected 17.84 units perinspection, compared with 8.28 for six independent runs of one policy, averaged across eight training seeds. With the same policies trained on 40-action routes, extending each inspection route to 80 and 120 actions raised coverage to 22.52 and 28.38 units. Across seven public discrete-state tasks, CoverShare increased state support by a mean of 16.29% over the team-entropy baseline. Controlled ablations identified leave-one-policy-out coverage as the main contributor to these gains.

replace-cross Towards Efficient Auto-Interpretability of Boltz-1 Sparse Autoencoders

Authors: Piotr Jedryszek, Oliver M. Crook

Abstract: Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) could turn AI models in Biology and other fields into engines of scientific discovery by explaining the superhuman capabilities of those models. We postulate that a latent is useful if we know three things: whether it is coherent, whether it can be described, and whether that description has predictive power. These questions are routinely conflated. We assemble them into a single pipeline and report some practical innovations. First, cross-seed dictionary stability prioritises which latents are worth spending resources to investigate. Second, an intruder-detection task asks whether a latent's activating examples share a recognizable pattern. Third, a separate pass proposes a candidate biological description which we convert into falsifiable predictions which can be tested in silico. Deployed on the Boltz-1 Pairformer trunk, stability prioritisation finds interpretable latents using about 4.4 times fewer latent evaluations each, and at 5.2 times lower measured LLM cost, while recovering over half of them. However, we notice that in our setup the LLM judge is heavily biased towards consistent residue identity and a simple filter that selects for features with high amino-acid identity precision outperforms the stability thresholding. This simpler method has the added benefit of requiring no extra SAE seeds but it remains uncertain if it reflects a genuinely useful heuristic or an limitation of the LLM judge.

replace-cross Representation Learning with Quantum Signal Processing

Authors: Junqi Wang, Junyu Liu

Abstract: Representation learning begins when training changes the features that define similarity between data. A frozen-kernel model only reweights a fixed geometry. We establish quantum signal processing (QSP) as a solvable quantum model of the representation-learning regime. At arbitrary depth, we compute the exact mean and variance of its quantum neural tangent kernel, revealing an input-dependent angular geometry whose diagonal remains non-self-averaging even when the underlying unitary approaches Haar randomness. We also prove a sparse-data guarantee for the full nonlinear gradient flow without freezing or ensemble-averaging the kernel: the realized dynamics converges to an integrable scalar flow with a time-dependent kernel closure and explicit convergence times. A finite-depth speed limit holds for every data set and trajectory. At higher data density, numerical results show coupled evolution beyond both the scalar and frozen-kernel descriptions. These results give a controlled theory of learned quantum data geometry with provable training dynamics beyond the frozen limit.

replace-cross Arabic Safety Alignment as Selective Refusal: An Empirical Study of SFT, DPO, and Guard Calibration

Authors: Mohamad Zbib, Mohamad Bazzi, Ammar Mohanna

Abstract: Arabic large language models must refuse harmful prompts without over-refusing benign or sensitive prompts, yet a single refusal rate hides this trade-off. We evaluate it using benign refusal B and harmful-prompt refusal H, where H measures refusal rather than harmful compliance. Across five Arabic-capable models and 130 runs on the full human-written AraSafe set, refusal-only supervised fine-tuning (SFT) collapses toward blanket refusal, whereas selected mixed-SFT configurations reach H = 90% to 93% at B = 14% to 23%; four selected configurations exceed the H = 90% target in all three runs, while Fanar does so in two of three. Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) and inference guards change B and H differently across models rather than acting as uniform upgrades. In a blinded 300-response audit, annotator binary-refusal agreement is 89.0% (kappa = 0.78); Qwen3Guard and Aya Expanse 32B reach 88.7% and 91.0% accuracy, respectively, with no conclusive paired difference. Selected SFT raises H on Arabizi for all five models, but none reaches 90%, showing only partial transfer from Modern Standard Arabic. Overall, the results support model-specific operating-point selection: set a deployment target and retain only interventions that improve it.

replace-cross The Safeguard Worked. Is the LLM System Safer?

Authors: Pingyu Wu, Weiming Zhang, Nenghai Yu

Abstract: Safeguards for large language model services are typically evaluated by their attack-blocking rates on test suites. Deployment decisions also depend on how much harmful assistance attackers can still obtain from the system, which these rates do not measure. To determine what published evaluations establish about deployment safety, we develop a framework that translates reported results into the minimum and maximum harmful assistance that can remain under a specified outcome and threat model. For safeguards that block attacks through a check, we show that the most an attacker can obtain depends on three properties: whether attack paths pass through the check, its miss rate against every attacker in the threat model, and how much assistance remains after a block. We apply this framework to 85 safeguard evaluations from 52 papers. Of these, only five argue that every attack path passes through the check, and four provide evidence on what remains after a block. These evidence gaps have practical consequences: in NeMo Guardrails' default streaming configuration, the check blocked 94% of password leaks in our test, yet the client received the password in every leak, blocked or not. We identify which of the three properties each safeguard category's evaluations omit, and what to measure for each of four ways attacks bypass a check. Deployments that require these measurements before launch can choose and configure safeguards that limit what attackers obtain from the deployed system.

replace-cross Amortizing Scaling Law Construction Costs

Authors: Abhash Kumar Jha, Diana Alexandra Onu\c{t}u, Neeratyoy Mallik, Swagatam Haldar, Sam Laing, Niccol\`o Ajroldi, Shiwei Liu, Joaquin Vanschoren, Aaron Klein

Abstract: Scaling laws guide the design choices for training large foundation models, but deriving them involves training an exhaustive grid over hyperparameters, token budgets, and parameter counts, which is computationally expensive. Fitting a scaling law, however, only requires the best-loss frontier across compute scales, discarding most of the trained configurations. We propose a framework for efficient scaling law construction that formulates data collection as a Bayesian optimization problem, and introduce metrics for comparing scaling law fitting methods under constrained compute budgets. We find that progressively expanding the compute budget during acquisition, mirroring the compute-ordered evaluation of configurations in practice, substantially improves recovery efficiency. Augmenting the observed configurations with surrogate-fantasized evaluations then recovers the broader experimental grid, allowing accurate scaling law fitting without training every configuration. Together, these can closely match scaling law fits over a full dense grid at computational savings of up to $10\text{--}100\times$.

replace-cross Structure Across Voices: Comparing acoustic-event type accumulation and sequence dependence across four vocal corpora using frozen audio encoders

Authors: Mudit Sinha, Sanika Chavan

Abstract: Comparing acoustic structure across very different vocal corpora is difficult because the recordings use different native event units, contain different amounts of sequence, and were collected under different conditions. We use the high-dimensional representations of frozen, pretrained audio encoders to measure acoustic diversity and temporal organization. Matched sampling gives each corpus the same opportunities to observe new acoustic forms and relationships between events, without changing its native event definition. We compare sperm-whale codas, human speech phones, Bengalese-finch syllables, and common-marmoset calls using 1,952 events and 1,700 adjacent pairs per comparison. Whale shows the fastest accumulation of encoder-defined acoustic groups, while Finch has the strongest repeated-sequence recurrence against ordinary within-block shuffles. Their neighboring-event dependence is closely matched under a source-and-position control. A second preceding event changes held-out prediction more favorably for Whale than for the other corpora, although Whale's standalone improvement remains uncertain. Waveform measurements recover several differences, but duration and acquisition-sensitive properties also contribute. Together, the findings show how pretrained representations can measure distinct forms of vocal organization, while waveform and source controls define the scope of the comparison.

replace-cross Uncovering Faithfulness Gaps in VLMs via Explanation-Driven Counterfactual Testing

Authors: Sihao Ding, Santosh Vasa, Aditi Ramadwar, Thomas Monninger

Abstract: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) can produce Natural Language Explanations (NLEs) that sound plausible yet remain inconsistent with the visual evidence they cite. We present Explanation-Driven Counterfactual Testing (EDCT), an intervention-based protocol that extracts visual concepts cited in a model's explanation, applies verified minimal edits to them, and tests whether the resulting answer and explanation remain consistent with the edited image. Using this protocol, we create EDCT-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark spanning three complementary domains: knowledge-intensive visual question answering (OK-VQA), safety-critical driving (DriveLM), and 3D spatial reasoning (3DSRBench). Across the evaluated VLMs, EDCT reveals substantial faithfulness gaps, with models frequently producing responses inconsistent with verified visual changes. Finally, our fine-tuning study suggests that EDCT-generated counterfactuals provide high-impact training signals.

replace-cross RPMem: Learning Long-Term Recurrent Parametric Memory Across Sessions for LLM Agents

Authors: Fanyu Zhao, Ruike Cao, Liang Dong, Fugen Yao, Jian Xu, Guanjun Jiang, Yifei Zhao, Yinsheng Li, Han Zhang

Abstract: Long-running LLM agents require memory that persists and evolves across sessions. Text-based memory retrieves and reconstructs past interactions at every query, making long-horizon performance increasingly dependent on retrieval quality and contextual reasoning as histories grow. Parametric memory encodes experience directly into model computation, but existing approaches provide limited support for cross-session memory evolution. Their coupling to a specific backbone further restricts memory reuse after model replacement. We introduce RPMem, a two-stage architecture that compiles each session into a model-independent latent memory through forward computation and selectively integrates it with retained memory via a task-trained recurrent gate. The consolidated memory is then mapped to backbone-specific low-rank adaptation (LoRA) parameters, allowing the encoding capability to transfer when the backbone is replaced. Evaluation across three long-term memory benchmarks and five diverse backbones demonstrates broad generalization with near-constant update cost and memory footprint. With Qwen3-8B on PERMA, RPMem reaches 85.52%, outperforming the strongest parametric and text-based baselines by 5.32 and 12.98 percentage points, respectively. Ablations validate the complementary roles of session compilation and cross-session consolidation, while dynamics analyses reveal that the gate acquires task-specific memory integration strategies. These results establish RPMem as a lifecycle-independent parametric memory framework that maintains evolving cross-session memory that remains reusable across backbone replacements. Our code is available at https://github.com/Quark-Medical/rpmem/tree/main.

URLs: https://github.com/Quark-Medical/rpmem/tree/main.

replace-cross FLARE: A Full-Lifecycle Dense Supervision Paradigm for Long-Horizon Coding Agents via Generative Reward Model

Authors: Jingxuan Xu, Gang Wu, Yanan Wu, Yutao Mou, Songwei Yu, Tianzhuang He, Zhengshuo Gong, Zhao Liu, Zihang Xu, Wenqiang Zhu, Xinping Lei, Weihao Li, Ziqi Miao, Yuhui Bai, Zhongqiu Wang, Yan Wu, Ariel Deng

Abstract: While test-time scaling enhances Large Language Model (LLM) agents in long-horizon software engineering (SWE), sparse binary rewards (Pass/Fail) create a severe credit assignment crisis and waste failed exploratory trajectories. Current trajectory optimization and scaling methods are costly and structurally limited, relying on heuristic state reuse without causal diagnosis or delayed scalar scoring without actionable online guidance. We propose FLARE (Full-Lifecycle Alignment and Reward Engine), a novel dense supervision paradigm driven by a lightweight Generative Reward Model (GRM). First, RADAR, an offline causal-aware diagnostic framework, extracts high-fidelity, hindsight-free supervision through causal-chain backtracking to distill a GRM providing real-time, step-level risk feedback. Second, FLARE uses this GRM to continuously optimize the agent across its entire lifecycle. During inference, FLARE acts as an Active Scaffold, autonomously intercepting high-risk generation steps for localized breakpoint re-execution, drastically reducing compute overhead. During post-training, the GRM's structured signals serve as process-supervised reranking scores for Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and step-level dense rewards for Reinforcement Learning (RL), mitigating policy collapse in sparse environments. Extensive evaluations show that FLARE establishes a new Pareto frontier across the agent lifecycle: FLARE (N=1) outperforms Global Rollout (N=5) with a 5x reduction in token consumption. Extending FLARE to training overcomes the sparse reward problem in long-horizon interactive tasks, delivering relative performance gains of 19.13% in SFT through process-aware data curation and a consistent 9.19% improvement in RL.

replace-cross Video-HopChain: Multi-Hop Questions and Confidence-Gated Exploration for Video Reasoning Models

Authors: Trung Nguyen Quang, Yuhao Dong, Shuo Sun, Shuai Liu, Shulin Tian, Kim-Hui Yap, Ziwei Liu

Abstract: HopChain has shown on still images that multi-hop data synthesis improves vision-language reasoning, because long chain-of-thought reasoning exposes errors that compound across steps, while most data used for reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) rarely demands a chain of visual evidence, so these weaknesses are likely to stay unexposed. We observe the same problem in video, where this framework has not yet been explored. We therefore build Video-HopChain, a dataset of 22,550 multi-hop video questions over 13,378 videos, together with a held-out benchmark of 1,000 questions. Each question chains three to six yes/no questions about moments in one video, and each yields one of two integers depending on its answer. The final answer is the sum of these integers, so an exact match on that sum gives the verifiable reward that RLVR needs. We first train Qwen3-VL-8B with GRPO on a standard video dataset, and a second stage on Video-HopChain then raises the mean over eight video understanding and reasoning benchmarks from 55.4 to 57.9 and improves every one of them. Training on such a dataset, however, exposes a known limitation of GRPO: its learning signal comes from the reward variance within a group, so hard questions whose rollouts are all incorrect and easy questions whose rollouts are all correct both leave the group with no gradient. To recover these groups at the same compute budget, we introduce Confidence-Gated Exploration (CGE). With 8 rollouts per question, CGE samples the first 4 as usual. If these 4 are either all correct or all incorrect, it samples the last 4 with the policy's most confident token masked inside the reasoning span, and removes the masked positions from the loss while all 8 rollouts enter the advantage. With CGE, the mean rises further to 59.3. We release the dataset, the checkpoint, and the data generation and training code.

replace-cross Intuition Vectors from Pretrained Representations Steer Diffusion-Based Reasoning

Authors: Shahar Haim, Daniel C. McNamee

Abstract: Abstract visual reasoning requires identifying relationships between few examples and applying them to novel inputs. Recent advances have been driven by large language and vision-language models or specialized domain-specific architectures. It remains unclear whether abstract reasoning can be supported directly by the relational geometry of pretrained visual representations, without relying on language-based inference or task-specific representation learning. We investigate this possibility using frozen self-supervised vision encoders. With latent vector arithmetic, we confirmed that the representational geometry of two self-supervised encoders support abstract concept discrimination on Bongard problems and encode systematic relationships between input-output pairs in ARC tasks. Simple vector differences between image embeddings, which we call intuition vectors, capture transformations that generalize across instances of the same task. These vectors distinguish among 794 abstract reasoning tasks with up to 87.2\% accuracy, preserve relational structure across task-invariant visual transformations, and guide output retrieval for unseen inputs. Given this, we tested the hypothesis that this relational structure can steer generative reasoning by conditioning a diffusion transformer operating in pretrained visual latent space. On held-out ARC tasks, intuition-vector conditioning outperforms conditioning on complete demonstrations while requiring nearly six times less test-time compute, and supports zero-shot inference. Taken together, our findings suggest that pretrained visual representations provide a transferable substrate for abstract reasoning, enabling a modular approach in which the relational structure inferred from demonstrations steer generative reasoning.

replace-cross SkillPE: Creativity-Oriented Cinematic Skill Evolution for Text-to-Video Prompt Enhancement

Authors: Yanwei Huang, Mingxuan Zhu, Shujie Li, Shiyuan Liu, Yuanxing Zhang, Arpit Narechania

Abstract: Achieving high-quality cinematic text-to-video generation remains challenging for non-experts, whose prompts often lack professional narrative and creative design. Existing prompt enhancement (PE) methods and static cinematic skills offer limited capabilities for exploring diverse creative realizations. We propose SkillPE, a creativity-oriented PE framework that systematically evolves reusable cinematic skills through reference-guided exploration. Starting from expert-provided cinematic knowledge, SkillPE initializes and normalizes 20 structured seed skills. It then retrieves movie references categorized as resonators (good matches), dissonants (weak matches), and divergents (creatively inspiring near-misses). Resonators and dissonants refine skill applicability and execution, while divergents inspire alternative cinematic realizations through controlled mutations that preserve user intent. Candidate skills are assessed through generated videos along prompt fidelity, cinematic quality, narrative appeal, and creativity to construct the final skill libraries. Experiments on StoryEval and VBench demonstrate improvements of up to 1.10 points over seed skills and 1.02 points over the strongest external baseline on a 7-point four-dimensional evaluation scale. Overall, SkillPE offers a practical approach to balancing fidelity and creativity in cinematic text-to-video generation.

replace-cross Representable but Unlearned: Encoding Rank and the Interaction-Prediction Floor

Authors: Zahra Khodagholi, Niloofar Yousefi

Abstract: Input encodings can prevent a predictor from reproducing measured interactions jointly, even when each interaction is individually representable. We compute the attainable contrast space from the encoder and contrast design, without labels or predictor fitting. Projecting measured contrasts onto this space gives an empirical squared-error floor for any deterministic decoder using the encoding. On an siRNA panel, a graph neural network's training-only feature mask merges 165 measured states into 90 classes, reducing the rank of 140 interaction contrasts to 72. The floor is 0.009980, or 14.63% of recorded contrast energy, the mean squared error of predicting zero for every contrast. Its 14.6% share of fitted interaction error adds little: predicted interactions are nearly zero, and their MSE of 0.068335 slightly exceeds that of the same control. Antisense-marginal shares are larger: 36.49% of recorded energy and 38.1% of fitted error. Three restored chemistry columns suffice for full rank. Removing the full mask eliminates the floor, but refitting improves interaction MSE by only 0.000017; the restored features remain absent from training inputs. Two further panels clarify the diagnostic's scope. A released splicing predictor encodes injectively and retains the full design rank of 1,986, so its floor is zero. For an independent count-fingerprint encoder on a lipid panel, 800 pairwise-distinct encoded contrast rows have rank 740, exposing 60 restrictions missed by duplicate-row checks. The diagnostic identifies encoding-imposed limits and can trace them to specific features. It separates representability from learned accuracy, but does not by itself predict refitting gains or explain the remaining error.

replace-cross LeapQuant: Efficient Linear Attention with Accurate Recurrent State Quantization

Authors: Yi Pan, Haocheng Xi, Kan Zhu, Xingyang Li, Yibo Wu, Mayank Mishra, Hongtao Zhang, William X. Zheng, Baris Kasikci, Song Han, Kurt Keutzer, Rishabh Iyer, Ion Stoica

Abstract: Recent LLMs increasingly adopt hybrid designs that replace standard attention with linear attention, such as Gated DeltaNet (GDN) and Kimi Delta Attention (KDA). Although they compress the context into a fixed-size recurrent state and substantially reduce the cost of long-context processing, repeatedly reading and updating that state remains a major inference bottleneck. Quantization offers a natural way to reduce this cost, but can significantly degrade model quality, due to the accumulation of rounding errors and the presence of outlier rows and columns in the state. To address these challenges, we propose LeapQuant, a training-free method that achieves near-lossless performance under 8-bit recurrent-state quantization. First, to mitigate error accumulation, we propose per-window quantization, which leaps over a window of tokens and quantizes the state only once at its end. Within a window, outputs are computed from the fixed low-bit state together with high-precision buffered updates. Second, to reduce the error introduced by each quantization, LeapQuant retains the state's largest outliers as a few high-precision Compensator Tokens, which share the update path of real tokens. We then smooth the remaining residual before quantization to further reduce the error. Comprehensive experiments across the Qwen, Kimi, and GLM model families show that LeapQuant substantially reduces memory and compute costs during inference. With accuracy comparable to the FP32 baseline, it achieves average speedups of 2.05--3.70$\times$ at the kernel level and 1.47$\times$ for end-to-end inference on NVIDIA B200, RTX PRO 6000, and RTX 5090 GPUs.

replace-cross Pseudo-Label-Triggered Retraining from Forecast Errors for Online Time Series Forecasting

Authors: Yeryeong Kwak, Yoo-Min Jung, Jonghun Park

Abstract: Real-world time series forecasting systems operate under non-stationary data streams, where forecasting performance may degrade over time. Although retraining can recover the performance, it incurs non-trivial computational and operational costs. Under limited deployment resources, the key challenge is therefore not only how to retrain but also when to retrain. While existing retraining policies often rely on indirect indicators such as drift alarms or model staleness, we instead use realized forecast errors as direct deployment feedback. In this paper, we propose PILOT (Pseudo-label-Informed Learned Online Trigger), an online retraining framework that learns when to retrain from forecast-error dynamics. Since ground-truth retraining labels are unavailable, PILOT constructs a pseudo-label from future increases in forecast error and trains a lightweight scorer to predict it from observed error states. At deployment, PILOT uses only completed forecast errors and serves as a plug-in module for arbitrary forecasting backbones without architectural modification. We evaluate PILOT under standard multivariate forecasting settings across eight benchmarks with three representative backbones---DLinear, iTransformer, and TimesNet. Across all three backbones, PILOT achieves state-of-the-art average-rank performance among retraining policies while maintaining a favorable performance--efficiency trade-off.

replace-cross Misalignment of Low-Loss Regions Causes Grokking

Authors: Yongding Tian, Zaid Al-Ars, Maksim Kitsak, Peter Hofstee

Abstract: Grokking refers to the delayed emergence of validation-set generalization after a model has already overfit the training set. Although first observed in small algorithmic tasks trained with transformers, its underlying mechanism remains unsettled. In this work, we develop an analysis framework based on mode connectivity and the geometry of low-loss regions. The framework predicts that the standard modular-arithmetic setting does not always produce grokking: under a symmetry-preserving train/validation split, we observe a stable anti-grokking case in which validation performance does not recover. This counterexample challenges several existing correlational explanations of grokking. More broadly, our analysis framework and results further suggest that grokking arises when the low-loss regions induced by the training and validation partitions are misaligned. Once these regions become well aligned, training hyperparameters alone cannot produce grokking and the observed dynamics collapse to either trainable or non-trainable behavior.

replace-cross AEGIS: Differentiable Mars Climate Model with Neural Closures

Authors: Sameera S Kashyap, Victor Cruz, Angel Yepez, Razvan Marinescu

Abstract: General circulation models (GCMs) are the primary tool for simulating planetary atmospheres. They play a vital role in understanding Mars's atmosphere, as forecasting its unique weather is mission-critical for operations such as entry, descent, and landing. Mars poses unusual challenges for these models, as observations are sparse compared to Earth. In addition, a thin $CO_2$ atmosphere alongside a radiatively active dust cycle creates a volatile atmosphere with large diurnal temperature swings and no true terrestrial analog for validation. Existing Mars GCMs, including the LMD PCM, the NASA Ames Mars GCM, and PlanetWRF, are mature and physically detailed but are implemented in legacy Fortran with finite-difference or finite-volume solvers, and they do not expose gradients for calibration or machine learning. Here we present AEGIS, a modular differentiable Mars climate model that couples Mars's unique atmospheric physics to the Dinosaur dynamical core, with interfaces for neural closures. We showcase stable ten-Mars-year simulations that reproduce the seasonal $CO_2$ cycle while conserving the total $CO_2$ inventory, capture realistic large-scale surface-temperature structure, and produce surface pressure that follows Mars Orbiter Laser Altimeter (MOLA) topography. Gradients through coupled trajectories agree with finite differences and support physical calibration and neural training. We compare with conventional GCMs, highlighting the framework's computational efficiency and differentiability.

replace-cross Can Power Draw Constrain Covert Compute? Limits of Analogue Verification for AI Governance

Authors: Tom Kimpson, Mauricio Baker, Emlyn Graham

Abstract: Frontier AI treaties or agreements on limiting computation require external verification; an external auditor must be able to confirm how much computation actually ran and that parties are adhering to the agreement. Analogue, off-chip measurements such as power draw provide an information channel for verification. It is unknown how well these analogue channels can constrain the amount of computation against an adversary who actively tries to subvert the audit. We derive a closed form for $\beta$, the largest hidden computation a power trace cannot exclude, as a fraction of the declared machine capacity. Measurements on NVIDIA A100 GPUs give $\beta = 1.16$ in the worst case, while an adversarial matched-energy schedule hides at least $0.41$. Analogue power measurements alone therefore constrain compute weakly. Additional restrictions granted by the threat model, such as the ability of the verifier to re-execute the declared work at an observed operating point, let the verifier push $\beta$ down to $0.059$ in the maximally restricted case. This gives a quantitative estimate of what analogue measurements can contribute to compute verification.

replace-cross Natural Code Transformation Confuses Agents in Vulnerability Analysis

Authors: Jie Zhu, Ronik Bhaskar, Heather Zheng, Ben Y. Zhao, Kexin Pei

Abstract: Having demonstrated a strong potential for automating vulnerability discovery, AI agents can also be used to attack software systems at scale. Today's software systems need mechanisms to level the playing field by slowing down potential attacks, giving them time to patch vulnerabilities and strengthen software. Along this line, our study of agent trajectories in vulnerability analysis shows a surprising finding: agents produce dramatically different results across real-world code commits that carry the same functionality and the same vulnerability. This phenomenon appears consistently in multiple code domains and vulnerability categories. It motivates us to study natural and functionality-preserving code transformations derived from real-world software development, and their potential as a defense against attacker agents. We build a corpus of natural transformations by mining recurring development patterns from real-world code commits, and apply them to evaluate impact on agentic vulnerability analysis. Our results show that even a single form of transformation can effectively "confuse" frontier agents, e.g., Claude Mythos 5.2 with Claude Code, reducing successes in vulnerability discovery. We also study different agent failures, whether transformations can be composed to provide stronger protection, and adaptive attackers who seek to reverse them. Overall, our study of natural code transformation significantly expands the research space of using code transformations to defend against malicious agents.

replace-cross SarcasTraps: Effect of Sarcasm on Turkish Hate Speech Detection

Authors: Niyazi Ahmet Metin, Dilara Kek\"ull\"uo\u{g}lu

Abstract: Standard hate speech detection datasets often emphasize explicit harmful language, but directed harm can also be expressed indirectly, without clearly hateful wording. Sarcasm is one such form of indirect expression, as it can convey harmful intent through mock praise, exaggeration, humor, or apparent politeness. In this paper, we introduce SarcasTraps, a targeted external test set for analyzing the effect of sarcasm on Turkish hate speech detection. SarcasTraps contains 479 entries annotated along two dimensions: hate speech and sarcasm. We train classifiers using BERTurk and XLM-RoBERTa on two existing Turkish hate speech datasets and evaluate them on SarcasTraps, using it strictly as an external test set. We also evaluate GPT-4.1 and Llama-3.3-70B in a zero-shot setting on SarcasTraps. Results show that sarcasm substantially affects hate speech detection. Fine-tuned encoder models lose recall on sarcastic hate, while zero-shot LLMs maintain higher recall but suffer substantial precision drops on sarcastic examples. These findings show that both fine-tuned hate speech detectors and zero-shot LLMs struggle when sarcasm is involved, and SarcasTraps provides quantitative evidence of this challenge through a targeted Turkish test set. Due to the presence of sensitive and hateful language, SarcasTraps will be shared upon request for academic research purposes. Warning: This paper has examples from the dataset which contains offensive and hateful content towards groups of people.

replace-cross Let the Mind Conceive, Let the Hand Achieve: Coupling Grounded Action and Progress Evaluation for Long-Horizon Manipulation

Authors: Youpeng Wen, Gang Wang, Tianyu Huang, Weiwei Zhang, Zhijian Huang, Wei Chen, Xieyuanli Chen, Yunhui Liu

Abstract: A robot following a multi-step instruction needs to act on the right objects in the right order, even as the scene changes. It must also maintain awareness of task progress and determine when to advance. To address these challenges, we introduce Orchestra, an agent harness that separates task planning and execution management from action generation. Orchestra decomposes a complete multimodal instruction into ordered subtasks, tracks completed steps, and updates the locations of relevant objects and destinations during execution. It maintains a shared context containing the active subtask, spatial grounding, and recent observations. An action policy uses this context to generate actions, while a separately trained progress evaluator estimates when to advance to the next subtask. To evaluate whether robots trained on atomic instructions and short trajectories can execute longer subtask sequences, we introduce a benchmark named RMBench-Compose. Orchestra achieves a mean success rate of $68.22\%$ across nine RMBench tasks, outperforming Mem-0 by $26.22$ percentage points. On RMBench-Compose, it achieves an Average Success Length (ASL) of $7.35$ subtasks, $2.46$ times that of HarnessVLA. Across four real-world multi-step tasks, it improves mean ASL by $25.0\%$ over Orchestra using the base $\pi_{0.5}$ policy. These results show that Orchestra helps robots carry out longer sequences of subtasks while preserving the requested subtask order and acting on the specified objects. Our code and project page are available at https://youpengwen.github.io/Orchestra

URLs: https://youpengwen.github.io/Orchestra

replace-cross Does On-Policy Distillation Need to Complete Every Rollout?

Authors: Zhengxin Zhang, Maosen Tang, Ning Wang, Changhao Li, Jingren Zhou, Bo Dai, Sainyam Galhotra, Alex Townsend, Claire Cardie

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) transfers teacher knowledge to a student through feedback on student-generated rollouts, but generating complete rollouts and computing per-token teacher feedback are computationally expensive. Existing truncation methods save computation by stopping rollouts early. However, they do not compensate for truncated token gradients, which biases the gradient estimates and changes Vanilla OPD's original training objective. Furthermore, their truncation decisions rely on assumptions about supervision quality or criteria that may not generalize across settings. We introduce Stochastic Truncated On-Policy Distillation (STOPD), which accelerates OPD by randomly truncating student rollouts and correcting the resulting sampling bias with blockwise inverse-probability weighting (IPW). At each training step, we sample a stopping position for each rollout before generation and let the student generate until that position or natural termination. We obtain teacher feedback on every generated token and use blockwise IPW to compensate for the lower probability of retaining later tokens while controlling the weights. We prove that this weighting preserves Vanilla OPD's gradient sum in expectation and derive one-step update-error bounds for SGD, Adam, and Muon. We evaluate STOPD across multiple teacher-student model pairs, task domains, and optimizers. STOPD reduces generated tokens by 25-48% while maintaining accuracy comparable to Vanilla OPD, achieving 1.3-2.2x training speedups.

replace-cross Forecasting Retail Sales at Every Grain: Trees, Foundation Models, Transformers and World Models Compared on Four Public Data Sets

Authors: Karthik Iyer, Madan Dabbeeru

Abstract: A retailer makes decisions from the same demand at different grains: store orders by item and day, promotions by item and week, category reviews and supplier terms. This paper asks whether one forecast, built at the finest grain and summed to every coarser grain, can serve them all, and which model does that best. It compares LightGBM, pretrained foundation models (TimesFM, Chronos-Bolt, Timestral), the Temporal Fusion Transformer and a world model that carries each item's state forward under the price and promotion plan, on M5, six Dominick's categories, Breakfast at the Frat and Corporacion Favorita, with the world model and LightGBM tuned by a common search. When to use the average: the average of the tuned world model and LightGBM, summed to every grain, has the lowest error in 22 of 27 grain and data set cases and is never the least accurate on the final held-out periods. When to use the world model: on existing items kept out of training it loses at most 0.040 in scaled error, against up to 0.311 for LightGBM, and on lines with short history it is clearly more accurate on six of nine data sets. When to use LightGBM: on heavily promoted lines, fast sellers and lines with long history (six of nine data sets each) and in price-cut weeks. Inconclusive: lines never promoted and slow lines are close to even, and 52 weeks ahead the average is the most accurate on five of seven data sets, with the world model ahead of LightGBM on two of seven. The findings hold for these data sets and the price and promotion levers; anyone adopting them should rerun every comparison on their own data.

replace-cross Activation-Space Exploration for Visual and Textual Reasoning with RLVR

Authors: Yunan Zeng, Jinhao Jing, Xi Cheng, Yuwang Wang

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) improves visual and textual reasoning by sampling responses, scoring them with an automatic verifier, and updating the policy from the resulting rewards. The policy therefore receives direct learning signals only from trajectories that appear in its rollouts. Yet repeated sampling from the same policy can concentrate on similar reasoning paths, leaving useful alternatives unexplored under a fixed rollout budget. We introduce Activation-Space Exploration (ASE), an online method for diversifying training rollouts. By clustering activation differences between correct and incorrect responses to the same problem, ASE builds a structured bank of latent exploration vectors. Different vectors are then applied to subsequent rollouts to explore alternative reasoning paths. Under the same rollout budget, ASE improves average accuracy over GRPO on Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct by 5.41 and 3.15 percentage points across six visual mathematical and six general visual reasoning benchmarks, respectively. On Qwen3-1.7B-Base, it improves average Mean@8 over GRPO across six mathematical reasoning benchmarks by 4.87 percentage points, with gains also observed on Qwen3-8B-Base.

replace-cross REGER: Modeling inter-item Relations with Semantic IDs for Generative Recommendation

Authors: Yuxi Huang, Silin Zhou, Chenhao Wang, Zihan Li, Gangming Guo, Pei Ke, Lisi Chen, Shen Gao, Shuo Shang

Abstract: Personalized recommendation depends on learning semantic and collaborative relations among items from online content and user interactions. Generative recommendation (GR) offers a promising paradigm by autoregressively generating discrete semantic IDs (SIDs) of items. However, randomly initialized SID token embeddings and GR models trained from scratch do not directly inherit the semantic relations inherent in items, while inter-item collaborative relations are learned largely implicitly through token-level attention and next-token supervision. To address this limitation, we propose REGER, a unified framework that explicitly incorporates inter-item relations into semantic pretraining, history encoding, and SID generation. We pretrain the GR model through generative semantic retrieval, enabling it to inherit inter-item semantic similarity by learning to generate semantic neighbors' SIDs. We combine global attention with local attention aligned with SID boundaries to capture historical context and directed transitions between adjacent items. We formalize item-level preference distillation on the SID prefix tree, deriving an exact factorization of item preference distributions into prefix conditionals and a weighted decomposition of item-level KL divergence into conditional KL terms. Guided by this decomposition, we renormalize the generator's probabilities over legal next tokens and match the resulting distributions to the teacher's conditional preferences along the ground-truth SID path, jointly optimizing the distillation objective with cross-entropy. Extensive experiments on real-world datasets validate the effectiveness of the proposed preference distillation and demonstrate that REGER consistently outperforms existing GR baselines while retaining efficient inference.

replace-cross Cassette-Editor: Shared Timeline Semantics for Human and Agent Editing

Authors: Zhixin Zeng, Jiahao Zhong

Abstract: Video editing involves tedious manual manipulation across interconnected timeline tracks, motivating automated agents that offload routine work while keeping the project directly revisable by humans. Fixed operation catalogs limit expressiveness, while even a basic trim requires coordinated updates across later clips, attached titles, and transitions. We present Cassette-Editor, an agent harness that unifies manual user gestures and automated agent patches within a shared editing environment. The shared action language expresses the editor's manual content changes, and a common history records committed edits for undo and manual refinement. When the model selects an edit, a state executor automatically derives its downstream consequences, such as shifting later clips and attached titles after a trim, while a validation rulebook enforces structural invariants and returns localized diagnostics for automated repair. To keep the agent grounded as the project evolves, dynamic read operations map transcripts and shot descriptions onto the current timeline. Evaluated with GPT-6 Luna, the full harness passed the scored request checks in 92/93 trials across 31 authored requests on two interview documentaries, compared with 48/93 for an internal basic-tools baseline. In ablations, disabling the consequences derived by the executor reduced passed trials from 230/232 to 199/232 on edits with dependent timeline elements, while ablating rulebook validation and repair lowered passed trials from 46/48 to 10/48 on requests operating near structural validity boundaries. Evaluating the complete system on 50 TVSum videos further demonstrated greater agreement with human annotations than a general agent using the same model (F1 23.8 vs. 20.1).

replace-cross WorldFlow: Joint Video and 3D Motion Prediction via Large-Scale Pretraining

Authors: Yuhao Lu, Jiongxu Chen, Hexiang Wei, Jiajin Wu, Juelin Li, Mingjian Liang, Fan Yang, Zhuowei Liang, Zihao Liu, Zhennan Wang, Sheng Wang, Lin Shao

Abstract: Predicting future physical states stands as a central challenge in embodied intelligence. Existing video world models leave 3D dynamics implicit, action-based formulations remain tied to specific robot configurations, and 3D motion prediction models use flow-only supervision, providing a limited learning signal for physical dynamics. To circumvent these limitations, we introduce \method{}, a generative world model that jointly predicts future video and query-based 3D trajectories. By parameterizing 3D motion as continuous displacement tokens aligned with the video grid, a unified Mixture-of-Transformers (MoT) backbone jointly denoises both modalities under a rectified-flow objective. This formulation couples explicit geometric dynamics with visual evolution, effectively unlocking the physical commonsense embedded within pretrained video models. To empirically scale and validate this unified architecture, we train \method{} on a heterogeneous mixture of 1.44 million object-level samples spanning simulated and real-world domains. We further introduce WorldFlowBench, comprising 1,678 evaluation samples from 11 data sources across simulated and real-world interactions, including spatial reasoning tasks. Across all three evaluation sets, WorldFlow achieves the lowest average displacement error (ADE) and final displacement error (FDE) among the evaluated learning-based methods. Notably, on the challenging SpatialCog set, WorldFlow achieves an ADE of 0.060 m, a 54.2\% reduction compared with the strongest external baseline. It also reduces ADE and FDE by 55.9\% and 68.5\%, respectively, compared with its flow-only variant. Project page: https://worldflow-rbs.github.io/.

replace-cross Multi-Estimate Elicitation Does Not Uniformly Debias Language Model Judgments

Authors: An-Sheng Jhang

Abstract: Multi-Candidate Verbalization (MCV) is a task- and model-dependent output protocol: repairing format does not guarantee better posterior accuracy, and requesting weights differs from reweighting an already generated candidate set. Across 31,672 planned/admitted evaluation units and 32,184 planned/admitted calls (excluding retries), we connect a frozen six-configuration cohort to exploratory scale/organization experiments and two independently preregistered post-cohort formal suites. Stage 1 retains 11 directional confirmations among 45 frozen tests; only increased post-outcome confirmation error on gpt-oss-120b exceeds its SESOI. P1 completes 1,920 terminal units: smaller response scales sharply lower operational string-match rates but increase Bayesian error. P2 completes 2,560 calls and 2,048 units: eliciting weights raises three errors by +2.75 / +2.05 / +3.88 pp, while within-generation weighting changes them by only -0.052 / -0.049 / -0.067 pp, a 42x-58x magnitude difference. replace-cross See, Think, Use: Diagnosing Perception and Reasoning Failures in VLMs

Authors: Jiacheng Ding, Yanliang Qi, Cong Guo, Peihua Mai

Abstract: We present See, Think, Use, a protocol that traces each answer of a vision-language model to perception, reasoning or the use of perception. The diagnosis shows a developer which stage to improve and directs test-time compute to the stage that fails. Benchmarks score the final answer, and an answer that rests on the image earns the same credit as a guess or a memorized prior. The protocol asks the model two extra questions per item and needs no access to its weights. A localization probe asks for a box around every instance and measures seeing, and a text-replacement probe answers from a description and measures thinking. Use is the rate at which the answer follows the model's own perception across counterfactual image pairs. The rate needs no answer labels, because the question and its prior are the same on both images. On two model families and six datasets the protocol shows that the knob that helps follows the task family. Answers follow the model's perception except on images that contradict a common prior, and asking the model to localize first repairs this failure when its boxes are right. Allocating compute by the measured stages comes close to the best uniform policy at lower cost and beats a random allocation of the same compute wherever a knob raises the short stage. We will release the code, the prompts and the per-question outputs.

replace-cross Malware Clustering in the Wild: Taxonomy, Challenges, and Evaluation Metrics

Authors: Raguvir S, Bojing Li, Prajna Bhandary, Charles Nicholas

Abstract: As malware continues to grow in volume, diversity, and sophistication, clustering has emerged as a critical technique for analyzing large malware corpora. Clustering enables the discovery of structural relationships, behavioral patterns, and evolutionary trends, and is widely used to group unknown samples, infer malware family boundaries, and identify novel variants. However, existing research remains fragmented, employing a wide range of clustering techniques with no unified perspective that systematically organizes prior work or clearly identifies gaps in the literature. This survey addresses this gap by organizing previous malware clustering research into seven categories: centroid-based, hierarchical-based, density-based, distribution-based, mixed- type, fuzzy, and AI-driven approaches. In addition, this survey summarizes publicly available malware datasets commonly used in clustering research and examines the feature representations derived from these datasets. Our goal is to clarify the current landscape and outline promising avenues for future research on malware clustering, including how AI-driven methods may shape large-scale and reproducible analysis.

Abstract: Natural Language Processing (NLP) has been widely applied in financial domains, enabling automated analysis and retrieval of stock market information. replace-cross EmoReward: Self-Evolving Reward Calibration for Emotional Video Generation RL

Authors: Mingzhe Zheng, Yue Wu, Jing Tan, Chang Zou, Dengyang Jiang, Zeyue Tian, Xuanhua He, Ryan Xu, Yunyang Ge, Sihui Ji, Yunlong Lin, Kaihang Pan, Hongru Yan, Sixiang Chen, Dianyi Wang, Julian Zhang, Weijie Kong, Changlin Li, Lanqing Guo, Zhao Zhong, Qifeng Chen, Harry Yang

Abstract: Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) has advanced generative model post-training in verifiable domains such as code and mathematics, but evaluating video quality, especially emotional expression, often depends on subjective aesthetics and has no uniquely correct target. Existing approaches typically rely on fixed rewards for visual quality, motion quality, and text alignment that poorly capture emotional intent and can invite reward hacking, while RLHF is difficult to scale and subject to individual aesthetic differences that complicate the calibration of facial, body motion, and chromatic cues across narrative contexts. We introduce EmoReward, a new self-evolving training mechanism that distills evidence reliability from reward traces collected during an actual training run to adaptively guide multi-reward weight allocation in subsequent training by determining which emotional evidence to trust and how much to weight it. EmoReward first plans specific grounding cues from the emotional prompt to guide evaluation, then uses heterogeneous signal primitives to score the generated video during the act stage. Reflective Reward Gating calibrates their contributions through instance-level gates for phase, face confidence, conflict, and subtlety, then renormalizes weights over the active set to produce the sample's fused reward. RewardMemory then estimates each primitive's reliability from rollout scores and outcomes within each emotional regime in the finished training run, then fixes bounded multipliers that calibrate fusion weights in the next training run without updating reward models. Experiments on HunyuanVideo 1.5 show improvements across multiple RL algorithms and compare EmoReward with other agentic reward methods under SAGE-GRPO. Ablations and transfer experiments on SD 3.5 and FLUX further support adaptive reward calibration for visual generation.

replace-cross Signed but Not Safe: Requirement Integrity in Multi-Agent LLM Handoffs

Authors: Xiaobo Chen, Genliang Zhu, Ziyuan Wang, Chu Wang

Abstract: Enterprise AI agents split requests across subagents, and each handoff can drop, alter or ignore a requirement. A preregistered study on synthetic, exactly scorable tasks compares free-text, structured JSON and signed requirement-manifest handoffs: the orchestrator extracts each requirement as a verbatim quote plus a checkable predicate, code signs it and deterministically verifies the merger's decision. With Qwen3-8B (1,440 trajectories, five threats), signed manifests cut unsafe commits under attack from 69.4% to 9.0% (60.4 pp, 95% CI [51.4, 69.4]) with no detectable utility loss versus structured handoffs (S2 -0.4 pp, 95% CI [-14.2, 13.3]). Signing defeats in-transit mutation: unsigned manifests violated 79.2 pp more often than signed ones, which never did; this replicated in gpt-oss-20b and gemma-4-12b. There, without an adversary, signed manifests were descriptively slightly worse than structured handoffs (11.1% vs 2.8%; 16.7% vs 8.3%; not tested) and lost utility overall (gpt-oss-20b, all threats: -28.7 pp). On identical Qwen3-8B trajectories, the deterministic verifier caught 78.7% of violating decisions versus 59.0% for an LLM checker, and an oracle manifest removed all remaining unsafe commits, so the residual risk is in extraction. A preregistered pre-signing injection confirmed this: one adversarial line raised signed-manifest unsafe commits from 14.6% to 37.5% (Qwen3-8B), 11.1% to 63.9% (gpt-oss-20b) and 16.7% to 97.2% (gemma-4-12b), and every such commit carried a manifest corrupted before signing. Signing protects transit; extraction is the trust boundary. A testable pre-signing specification (PSE), specified after the injection results and frozen before an offline evaluation, blocked all these injected commits (fixed templates) but refused 45.8%, 11.1% and 22.2% of benign requests, changing completions by -8.3 pp, -2.8 pp and 0.0 pp.

replace-cross Continued Training of Continuous Language Flows with Token Identity Squared Supervision

Authors: Jiawei Jiang, Zhaoxiang Zang, Zhengtao Yu

Abstract: Diffusion and flow models have recently been extended to language modeling, generating text through continuous denoising in token embedding spaces. Pretrained models provide token representations and contextual prediction capabilities, but improving generation with limited additional training requires an appropriate connection between supervision and continuous generation. When continuous predictions are formed by weighting token embeddings with predicted probabilities, different probability distributions can yield the same vector and are therefore indistinguishable under a continuous reconstruction objective based on mean squared error (MSE). We propose Token Identity Brier Flow (TIBF) to apply squared supervision directly to token identities. TIBF fixes token embeddings, combines network scores with token-matching scores under a Gaussian observation model, supervises token probabilities using the Brier loss, and uses probability-weighted embeddings for continuous generation. We show that, for fixed token embeddings, the squared error in token posterior prediction controls the squared errors of the continuous denoising prediction and the flow velocity. A risk decomposition separates uncertainty inherent in the observation from posterior fitting error, motivating continued training that preserves pretrained embeddings and focuses on probability prediction. From common pretrained initializations and under matched continuation budgets, TIBF achieves mean Gen.PPL values of 34.87 on OWT and 80.08 on LM1B, outperforming continuous-MSE and CE controls. Further evaluations show improvements across multiple sampling budgets. Code is available at https://github.com/Jiangjiawei-kust/TIBF.

URLs: https://github.com/Jiangjiawei-kust/TIBF.

replace-cross Goodhart in the Planner: Sampling-Based Control Exploits Rewards Learned from Human Preferences

Authors: Soovadeep Bakshi, Apurba Bose

Abstract: Robots increasingly plan by searching against a reward model learned from human preferences. We measure what this search does to the true objective. On HalfCheetah and Hopper with crowd-sourced labels, and on Meta-World drawer-open and plate-slide with synthetic and blind human labels, harder MPPI search cuts true return by up to 76% while held-out label accuracy stays at 0.75-0.97; with human labels the drawer opens in 0 of 20 runs, against 10 of 10 for the cloned policy the planner starts from. The effect persists with a learned dynamics model and an image scorer. We prove that $\chi^2$-regularized MPPI at a temperature set by the scorer's error spread on the proposals is worst-case optimal, and that no gain can be certified once that spread exceeds the scorer's own. Calibrated from 100 ranked proposal pairs, this rule is the safest fix we test, keeping 86-94% of the cloned policy's return on drawer-open and plate-slide.

replace-cross Attention at Absolute Zero

Authors: Mostafa 'Neo' Mohsenvand

Abstract: Attention scores candidate supports of an answer, which we call witnesses, and reads the scores at a temperature. We build a higher-order attention layer whose state lives on pairs of items: for a pair (a, b), each third item k is a witness judged through its legs (a, k) and (k, b). It reads witnesses at zero temperature, keeping the best and the number tied with it, passes on only bits comparing such counts, rebuilds its pair state from the input and these bits at every loop, and is trained from answers alone. Each choice removes one way soft attention fails at size: dispersed weight, unresolved gaps between learned scores, and a drifting soft state. In maximum retrieval it is right on at least 99.95% of sets 1,000 times larger than in training, on all ten seeds; trained with a soft count, the same network falls to 42% at 4,096 items. On CLRS-30 without hints, a general form of the layer (half its channels read at zero temperature, numbers embedded, state carried) beats the best published model, which uses hints on some tasks; the full cold layer is exact on every test input of 9 of the 12 tasks whose answers depend on numbers only through comparisons. From yes-or-no answers it decides 2-SAT and two-colouring exactly at 500 variables and, as our theory predicts, not parity equations. Beside a frozen 7B language model it chains family relations exactly at 1,000 statements after training on 16, where the model alone falls to 44%; finite checks on the weights prove every block that learned correct at every size, on acyclic contexts and given what the model reads. On CLUTRR it is right on every story of five to ten relations given the graphs, learning the 62 rules exactly; from text, with the model frozen, no hand-written rule and only a small read-in trained, it lifts an open 7B model from 33% to 65%, above every system we found without hand-written rules except frontier reasoning models.

replace-cross OverServe: Resource Amplification Attacks on SaaS Agents via Tool--Prompt Co-Design

Authors: Ruiheng Yu, Oteo Mamo, Siyu Gan, Weikuan Yu, Mulong Luo

Abstract: Advances in large language models (LLMs) are enabling software-as-a-service (SaaS) providers to automate longer business workflows with agents. In subscription-based SaaS deployments, model and tool calls consume provider resources without additional customer payments. Because the agent autonomously decides when to stop, a malicious tenant can send a prompt to the agent, exploiting its completion decisions to extend execution and waste provider resources. In this paper, we present OverServe, a multi-agent framework for constructing such attacks against SaaS agents. OverServe co-designs Model Context Protocol (MCP) status tools and completion-confirmation prompts for the attacker. Each tool call returns accurate status and terminates normally, yet the agent may repeatedly check an unfinished condition to satisfy the prompt. This consumes the SaaS provider's resources even after the original business task is completed. OverServe constructs service-specific tool--prompt pairs and uses separate tool and prompt reviews to guide candidate pair selection. Tested tool specifications trigger no alerts in the evaluated MCP scanners. We evaluate Qwen, Nemotron, DeepSeek, and Sonnet on selected tasks from nine benchmarks. All OverServe runs pass the original task checks. OverServe can reach a maximal amplification of API requests by $93.88\times$ while preserving the original task goal and collateral checks.

replace-cross Rethinking "RL Generalizes, SFT Memorizes": The Role of SFT Data

Authors: Yunlong Hou, Fengzhuo Zhang, Yuan Cheng, Jiachun Pan, Xingyao Li, Zhuoran Yang

Abstract: Large language models trained with Reinforcement Learning (RL) with verifiable rewards exhibit strong reasoning ability and broad generalization, whereas models trained with Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) are often viewed as more prone to memorization and limited transfer. This paper rethinks this distinction through the lens of SFT training data on widely used Qwen2.5 and Qwen3 series models. First, we study the role of data source and show that it is critical: a carefully mixed SFT dataset substantially outperforms data generated solely by a larger model. Second, we study the role of data scale and show that matching the number of correct rollouts between SFT and RL greatly improves SFT generalization, while matching the total rollout budget enables SFT to generalize as well as RL. Combining these two factors further enables SFT to generalize even better than RL. Third, by using LLM annotations to characterize the solution methods in training rollouts, we show that larger datasets cover more tail methods and that these tail methods provide generalizable reasoning signals. Finally, we support these empirical findings theoretically by analyzing the training dynamics of shallow transformers under both RL and SFT.

replace-cross ReShap: Interpretable Reinforcement Learning for High-Dimensional Multi-Action Settings

Authors: Yiyu Qian, Pengfei Guo, Liyuan Zhao

Abstract: Deep Reinforcement Learning (RL) plays a crucial role in tackling complex tasks, yet its reliance on deep neural networks makes the learned policies opaque. To enhance the trustworthiness of RL policies, existing work (e.g., the SILVER framework [Li et al., 2025]) has focused on Shapley-based interpretability, but is typically restricted to low-dimensional state spaces, binary-action settings, and a single class of linear surrogate. We propose ReShap, a policy-distillation framework that extends Shapley-based interpretability to high-dimensional, multi-action environments. ReShap extracts compact feature representations from raw image observations, performs SHAP-based feature attribution to characterize how each feature contributes to the policy's decisions, and distills the deep RL policy into an interpretable surrogate trained on the full set of (state, policy-action) pairs along the policy's visited distribution. We instantiate the surrogate with three interpretable model families (decision trees, linear regression, and logistic regression) and compare their fidelity and task return. We evaluate ReShap on Atari and Box2D environments using three deep RL algorithms and conduct a human-subject study to assess the clarity and trustworthiness of the resulting policies. Results show that ReShap preserves competitive task performance, that decision trees generally outperform linear and logistic regression, and that linear regression is weakest in most settings.

replace-cross Spec-Driven PBT: Deriving Semantic Invariants from Code Specifications for Agentic Property-based Testing

Authors: Aditya Shukla, Kartik Gupta, Ali Alavi, Pashootan Vaezipoor

Abstract: Software is increasingly written by AI agents that produce an implementation from a natural language specification, a practice known as agentic coding. Code is now produced faster than it can be reviewed, and has been shown to exhibit its own characteristic failure patterns. Property-based testing (PBT), a long-standing technique in which invariant properties of a program are checked against many generated inputs, has recently been revisited with LLM agents authoring the properties, thereby removing the high-effort derivation of invariants that has long hindered its adoption. Existing methods, however, derive these properties from the implementation alone (Code-driven PBT). At the same time, a thorough specification such as a product requirements document has become routine within agentic coding, yet its role in testing the resulting code remains underexplored. We study the value of the specification as a source of properties for testing agent-written code. Our key insight is that the specification, unlike the implementation, records what the code was meant to do, and invariants derived from it are not biased by particular implementation choices. We propose Spec-Driven PBT, an agent that first expresses invariants in natural language from the specification and only then inspects the implementation to translate them into concrete tests. Across 25 applications from ViBench, Spec-Driven PBT finds a novel set of bugs that Code-driven PBT misses, while writing 2.4x fewer property tests. Though the two approaches surface a roughly equivalent number of bugs, there is little overlap: nearly 60% of the 169 total bugs are found by only one. Qualitatively, we find that the bugs unique to Spec-Driven PBT tend to violate documented product workflows whereas those to Code-driven PBT instead concentrate on implementation robustness, offering complementary value for bug discovery in agent-written code.

replace-cross EgoX: Egocentric Cross-Embodiment Manipulation with Embodiment Dreaming

Authors: Yaru Niu, Binghong Chen, Changwei Yao, Shangtao Li, Zhenlong Fang, Revanth Krishna Senthilkumaran, Shuai Zhou, Yuemin Mao, Kalie Ching, Po-Ju Chao, Yudong Luo, Peiqi Yu, Heng Zhang, Hao Zhang, Bingqing Chen, Chen Qiu, H. Eric Tseng, Ye Yuan, Umar Iqbal, Changliu Liu, Jonathan Francis, Ding Zhao

Abstract: Generalist manipulation policies can benefit from the complementary experience of humans, real robots, and simulated agents. However, differences in morphology, sensing, and control interfaces make it difficult to learn effectively from their combined demonstrations. We introduce EgoX, an egocentric cross-embodiment learning framework that shares manipulation structure through fine-grained, semantically corresponding observation and action modalities while keeping sensing and control interfaces embodiment-specific. EgoX pretrains a Modular Embodiment Dreamer (MED) on this mixture and fine-tunes it on target-embodiment demonstrations. Within each embodiment, MED assigns a dedicated tokenizer to each observation modality and a dedicated expert to each action modality, with a Transformer trunk shared across embodiments. A training-only embodiment-dreaming objective predicts latent representations of future visual and proprioceptive states, encouraging the shared trunk to model each embodiment's dynamics. Across 13 task-embodiment pairs spanning five tasks and six robot embodiments, including five physical robots and one simulated humanoid, cross-embodiment pretraining raises MED's average normalized task progress from 0.63 to 0.80 over training from scratch. The gains are particularly pronounced under out-of-distribution conditions, where average task progress increases from 0.53 to 0.77. Ablations demonstrate the benefits of broader pretraining mixtures, modular interfaces, and embodiment dreaming, with dreaming producing more similar latent geometry across embodiments. Project page: egox-dream.github.io.

replace-cross RACE: Ranking-Uncertainty Aware Capacity-Adaptive Customization for Efficient Sequential Recommendation

Authors: Tianqi Liu, Kaipu Sun, Kairui Fu, Wenkai Wang, Sihao Liu, Hui Zang, Ran Chen, Tao Wang, Shengyu Zhang

Abstract: Efficient sequential recommendation has emerged as a promising paradigm for providing real-time personalized service. To deploy models under resource-constrained environment, model compression becomes a necessity, yet existing methods typically apply uniform compression ratios, overlooking the heterogeneity in user intent complexity. This "one-size-fits-all" approach leads to a fundamental Capacity-Complexity Mismatch, where computational resources are either wasted on simple instances or insufficient for complex ones. To address this, we propose RACE (Ranking-uncertainty Aware Capacity-Adaptive Customization for Efficient Sequential Recommendation), a framework that dynamically adapts both model structures and input sequences with uncertainty-aware compression ratios. To ensure capacity-adaptive inference, RACE captures the model's intrinsic uncertainty of its predictions and transforms it into a proactive resource allocator. Specifically, we derive a list-wise ranking-uncertainty metric from the distributional stability of top-$K$ predictions, which serves as a measurable proxy for instance complexity. A lightweight predictor is then trained via self-supervision to anticipate this uncertainty from early-layer features. Guided by this signal, RACE performs instance-specific slimming in two dimensions: a hypernetwork-style channel sensitivity extractor identifies parameter redundancy, while importance-aware progressive sequence filtering mitigates data redundancy. Extensive experiments on four popular datasets demonstrate that RACE outperforms state-of-the-art baselines on accuracy, efficiency, and adaptability.

replace-cross Gamer: A Generalist Vision-Language-Action Foundation Model for Long-Horizon Real-Time Game Agent

Authors: Run Luo, Kuan Zhang, Qi Deng, Yukun Chen, Zhihao Yang, Yufei Zhang, Renrong Hu, Yi Zhang, Guiyu Ma, Tianyu Fan, Longze Chen, Hao Sun, Jiaming Li, Jiaming Yang, Xiaohao Liu, Tao Tan, Minzheng Wang, Hangtian Jia, Shuai Yuan, Lvdi Wang, Xiao Liu, Chao Ma, Yuting Zou, Yinhe Zheng, Xiaobo Xia, Dawei Jiang, Shaoqing Ren

Abstract: We introduce Gamer, a generalist agent framework built for long-horizon tasks in complex interactive environments. Its end-to-end architecture combines perception, memory management, and multi-step reasoning. A shared training and deployment pipeline supports interaction with both conventional 2D graphical interfaces and complex 3D simulated worlds. We first continue pre-training on a dataset constructed from tens of thousands of hours of device-level keyboard and mouse interaction. These trajectories span simulated games and embodied environments, allowing the model to learn environmental dynamics across both physical and virtual domains.
